deal with the recursive nature of the functional equations being dealt
with, we proceed to see how the distributions/measures induced by
a formal solution of the functional equations in
t
can be computed
in closed form provided a certain linear operator on the space of
SB functions ends up being invertible. Both the invertibility of this
operator and the existence of unique solutions of the functional equations
in
t
hinge on whether or not the parameters of the F-series under
consideration satisfy a certain algebraic relation, the locus of solutions
of which I call the
breakdown varieties
of the F-series involved.
By the end, we establish
Theorem
12

(see page
12
), where it is shown that
the functional equations in
t
have a unique solution if and only
if the F-series’ parameters do not lie in the breakdown variety. Moreover,
whenever this unique solution exists, it is then necessarily the Fourier
transform of the F-series in question. In particular, given
\mathbf{n}
,
if we know Fourier transforms for
X_{\mathbf{m}}
for all
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
,
then we can use the functional equations in
t
to solve for a unique
formula for a Fourier transform of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
if and only if
the parameters of the
X_{j}
s do not lie in the breakdown variety
of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
.

As the computations in this paper show, given an algebraic variety,
we can construct an F-series/measure which will be degenerate for
a given choice of parameters if and only if those parameters lie in
the F-series’ breakdown variety. This, coupled with the results of
the current paper, hint at a greater structure present in the kinds
of measures we have been examining, one that can be used to detect
points in algebraic varieties and, more generally, to encode algebraic
varieties.
Section
5.2

deals with this issue as best as I currently can. After some examples
of the encoding in practice, w

show that, for F-series induced
distributions whose degeneracy/non-degeneracy encodes points of algebraic
varieties, the degeneracy is equivalent to the containment of the
distribution’s kernel in the image of a certain linear operator. Furthermore,
while the effects of pointwise multiplication on the varieties encoded
by two or more F-series still need to be explored in greater detail,
it is an easy and immediate consequence of
Proposition
38

that, when considering the vector space generated by finitely many
F-series distributions/measures, the natural tensor algebra structure
possessed by distributions/measures allows us to encode unions of
algebraic varieties. This is shown in
Proposition
39

on page
39
. There, we have that if
V_{1},\ldots,V_{d}

are the breakdown varieties of the F-series distributions
dM_{1},\ldots,dM_{d}
,
then
V_{1}\cup\cdots\cup V_{d}
is the breakdown variety of the
distribution
dM_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes dM_{d}
.

Section
5.3
, the
paper’s final section, is primarily speculative. The original impetus
behind my PhD dissertation was to explore certain arithmetic dynamical
systems. One of the principal results of my dissertation was that,
in many cases, given the arithmetical dynamical system generated by
what I call a
hydra map
, one can construct an F-series, which
I call the
numen
,

whose value distribution properties
control the dynamics of the associated hydra map, such as the map’s
periodic points and cycles.
Theorem
2

shows that the numen of a hydra map can be realized as a measure,
and open the door for the application of Fourier-theoretic techniques
to study the dynamics of hydra maps by way of their numens. Together,
these two observations suggest it might be possible to improve our
knowledge of hydra maps’ dynamics by fleshing out the dictionary between
algebraic varieties and F-series and then using this dictionary to
study the breakdown varieties encoded by their numens. To that end,

Section
5.3

explores
a handful of examples of how algebraic varieties appear to be intertwined
with hydra maps’ numens, in the hopes inspiring new avenues of future
work.

Note 3
.

Throughout this paper, I have strived to make my exposition as accessible
as possible. An advanced undergraduate mathematics education and/or
early graduate-level education should be more than sufficient preparation.

2.
The Big Idea

After many failed attempts at alternative structures, I’ve found that
the cleanest way to go about the proof is to do everything formally,
without regard to delicate issues of convergence, so as to make the
argument as clear as possible. In this section, I will present the
computational arc of the proof, while marking those steps where more
thorough justification is needed.

For psychological reasons, I recommend that readers turn off their
brains for a while and
focus on the formal, symbol-pushing computation
.
The nature of this work is best understood as coming up with analysis-based
justifications for results that should arguably be provable in a purely
algebraic context.

Assumption 3
.

THROUGHOUT THIS SECTION, WE WORK WITH A FIXED GLOBAL FIELD
K
AND
AN INTEGER
p\geq 2
. IF
K
HAS POSITIVE CHARACTERISTIC, WE REQUIRE

\textrm{char}K
TO BE CO-PRIME TO
p
.

2.1.
M-functions

In the work to come, we will often find ourselves dealing with a product
of the form:

(2.1)

\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}

where
r_{0},\ldots,r_{p-1}\in K
are constants in a global field,
and where
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
The notation is as follows: for each
\mathfrak{z}
and each
k
,
compute the
p
-adic integer
\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
,
and then set
j_{k}
to be the projection of
\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

mod
p
. Then:

(2.2)

\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{j_{k}}

We can compute this product in closed form by scrutinizing the
p
-adic
digits of
\mathfrak{z}
.

Proposition 2
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

and
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Then:

(2.3)

\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}=r_{0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(r_{j}/r_{0}\right)^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}

Proof: We begin by writing
\mathfrak{z}
as a Hensel series:

(2.4)

\mathfrak{z}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}d_{j}p^{j}

Here:

(2.5)

\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}=\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}d_{j+k}p^{j}\right]_{p}=d_{k}

and so:

(2.6)

\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{d_{k}}

Here, we note that for any
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,p-1\right\}
, the
number of
k
in
\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}
for which
d_{k}=j

is equal to
\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)
.
Thus:

(2.7)

\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}=r_{0}^{\left|\left\{k\in\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}:d_{k}=0\right\}\right|}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}r_{j}^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}

Now, let
k_{n}
be the integer so that
n-k_{n}-1
is the largest
index
\ell\in\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}
for which
d_{\ell}\neq 0
.
That is:

\displaystyle d_{n-1}=d_{n-2}=\cdots=d_{n-k_{n}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle d_{n-k_{n}-1}

\displaystyle\neq 0

Observe that
\lambda_{p}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)=n-k_{n}
,
while:

\displaystyle\left|\left\{k\in\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}:d_{k}=0\right\}\right|

\displaystyle=k_{n}+\lambda_{p}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=n-\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

Thus:

(2.8)

\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}=r_{0}^{n-\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}r_{j}^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}=r_{0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(r_{j}/r_{0}\right)^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}

Q.E.D.

We can characterize expressions of the form (
2.1
)
as follows:

Proposition 3
.

Let
R
be an integral domain,
and let
\left\{M_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 0}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p},R\right)
.
Then, the following are equivalent:

I. There exist
r_{j}\in R\backslash\left\{0\right\}
for
j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

so that:

(2.9)

M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}

II. The following two properties hold:

i. For each
n
,
M_{n}\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p},R\right)

is locally constant mod
p^{n}
and has no zeroes.

ii.

(2.10)

M_{n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)=\frac{M_{m+n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}{M_{m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall m,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

where both sides are contained in
R
.

III. There exist
r_{j}\in R\backslash\left\{0\right\}
for
j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

so that:

(2.11)

M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=r_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}M_{n-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right),\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 1

Remark 12
.

The integral domain condition on
R
is needed to ensure that
r_{0},\ldots,r_{p-1}\in R\backslash\left\{0\right\}

guarantees that
M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\neq 0
for all
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Proof:
• (II
\Rightarrow
I) Suppose (II) holds. For each
j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
,
let
r_{j}
denote
M_{1}\left(j\right)
. Then, for
j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

and
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, note that
\theta_{p}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)=\mathfrak{z}
.
Thus (ii) for
m=1
gives:

(2.12)

M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\frac{M_{n+1}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)}{M_{1}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)}

and so:

(2.13)

M_{n+1}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)=M_{1}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right),\textrm{ }\forall j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 0

Since
M_{1}
is locally constant mod
p
, we have
M_{1}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)=M_{1}\left(j\right)=r_{j}
,
and thus, that:

(2.14)

M_{n+1}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)=r_{j}M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right),\textrm{ }\forall j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 0

Setting
\mathfrak{y}=p\mathfrak{z}+j
, we get:

(2.15)

M_{n+1}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)=r_{\left[\mathfrak{y}\right]_{p}}M_{n}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right),\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 0

Setting
n=\mathfrak{y}=0
, we get:

(2.16)

\underbrace{M_{1}\left(0\right)}_{r_{0}}=r_{0}M_{0}\left(0\right)

Since
r_{0}\neq 0
, this forces
M_{0}\left(0\right)=1
. Since
M_{0}

is locally constant mod
p^{0}
, it is a constant function, and thus

M_{0}=1
. Consequently, by induction:

\displaystyle M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\left[\mathfrak{y}\right]_{p}}M_{n-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\left[\mathfrak{y}\right]_{p}}r_{\left[\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right]_{p}}M_{n-2}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ 2}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{M_{0}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right)}_{1}\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right]_{p}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right]_{p}}

Thus, (I) is satisfied.
\checkmark

•(I
\Rightarrow
II) Suppose (I) holds. Then, by
Proposition

2
, we have:

(2.17)

M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=r_{0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(r_{j}/r_{0}\right)^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}

This shows that
M_{n}
is non-vanishing and locally constant mod

p^{n}
, proving (i). As for (ii),
Proposition
1

can be written as:

(2.18)

\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if }n=0\\
\#_{j}\left(\left[\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p^{n-1}}\right)+\left[\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}=j\right]&\textrm{if }n\geq 1\end{cases},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

Hence, by induction:

(2.19)

\#_{j}\left(\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p^{n-m}}\right)=\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\left[\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}=j\right],\textrm{ }\forall m\in\left\{0,\ldots,n\right\}

So, adding
m
to
n
gives:

\displaystyle\#_{j}\left(\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n+m}}\right)-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\left[\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}=j\right],\textrm{ }\forall m\in\left\{0,\ldots,n+m\right\}

\displaystyle=\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n+m}}\right)-\#_{p:j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{m}}\right),\textrm{ }\forall m\in\left\{0,\ldots,n+m\right\}

Hence:

\displaystyle M_{n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=r_{0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(r_{j}/r_{0}\right)^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle=r_{0}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(r_{j}/r_{0}\right)^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n+m}}\right)-\#_{p:j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{m}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{0}^{n+m}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(r_{j}/r_{0}\right)^{\#_{j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n+m}}\right)}}{r_{0}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(r_{j}/r_{0}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{m}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{m+n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}{M_{m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}

and so, (II) is satisfied.

•(II
\Leftrightarrow
III) This equivalence is obvious,
and follows immediately from the arguments given in (II
\Rightarrow

I).

Q.E.D.

This justifies the following definition:

Definition 9
.

Given an integral domain
R
and an integer
p\geq 2
, a
p
-adic

R
-valued M-function
is a sequence of functions
\left\{M_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 0}

in
\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p},R\right)
satisfying either of
the two equivalent conditions of
Proposition
3
.
The constants
r_{0},\ldots,r_{p-1}\in R\backslash\left\{0\right\}

given in
Proposition
3

are called the
multipliers
of the M-function.
r_{0}
is
called the
zero multiplier
.

The
\kappa
-factor
of an M-function
\left\{M_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 0}

is the function
\kappa:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\textrm{Frac}R

defined by:

(2.20)

\kappa\left(n\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{r_{j}}{r_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(n\right)}

where the
r_{j}
s are the multipliers of the M-function.

Because M-functions are uniquely determined by their zero multipliers
and
\kappa
-factors, we will sometimes give M-functions as a pair

\left(r,\kappa\right)
, where
\kappa
is a
\kappa
-factor, and

r
is the zero factor.

Furthermore, we write
\textrm{MFunc}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p},R\right)

to denote the
set of all
R
-valued
p
-adic M-functions
.
An element of
\textrm{MFunc}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p},R\right)
is written
as a sequence
\left\{M_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 0}
.

Remark 13
.

Proposition
3
then
implies that every M-function can be uniquely written as:

(2.21)

M_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=r_{0}^{n}\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right),\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 0,\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

where
r_{0}
is the zero multiplier and
\kappa
is the
\kappa
-factor.

Proposition 4
.

Let
\kappa
be the
\kappa
-factor
of an M-function. Then:

(2.22)

\kappa\left(\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p^{n}}\right)=\frac{\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{m+n}}\right)}{\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{m}}\right)}

Proof: Equivalent to the proof of (
2.10
).
Q.E.D.

Remark 14
.

Equation (
2.10
) from
Proposition

4
is incredibly important, and will
be used repeatedly in nearly everything that follows.

2.2.
The Central Computation

We fix once and for all integers
d\geq 1
,
p\geq 2
, and a global
field
K
. If
K
has positive characteristic, we require
\textrm{char}K

to be co-prime to
p
.

Now we introduce the notation to be used for the rest of this section
and for a good deal of the rest of the paper.

Notation 1
.

For each
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
and each
k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
,
let
a_{j,k}
and
b_{j,k}
be indeterminates, completely unrelated
to one another. Fix a
K
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
containing the
a_{j,k}
s
and
b_{j,k}
s so that, for each
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
,
there is a degree
1
F-series
X_{j}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}

satisfying:

(2.23)

X_{j}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)=a_{j,k}X_{j}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{j,k},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

Finally, given any
d
-tuple
\mathbf{n}=\left(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}

of non-negative integers, we write:

(2.24)

X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1}^{d}X_{j}^{n_{j}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

where
X_{j}^{n_{j}}
is defined to be the constant function
1

for any
j
for which
n_{j}=0
. We then write:

(2.25)

a_{\mathbf{n},k}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1}^{d}a_{j,k}^{n_{j}}

(2.26)

b_{\mathbf{n},k}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1}^{d}b_{j,k}^{n_{j}}

We write
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
to mean the
\mathbf{m}=\left(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}\right)

is a
d
-tuple of non-negative integers so that
m_{j}\leq n_{j}

for all
j
; we write
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
to mean
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}

and there exists at least one
j
so that
m_{j}<n_{j}
. We also
write:

(2.27)

\binom{\mathbf{n}}{\mathbf{m}}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\binom{n_{j}}{m_{j}}

and:

(2.28)

\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}

Remark 15
.

\mathcal{A}
is our stand-in for whatever the correct choice of
codomain will turn out to be for our F-series.

Our goal is to show that if each
X_{j}
has a Fourier transform,
then so does
X_{\mathbf{n}}
for all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
.
To do this, we begin by establishing
X_{\mathbf{n}}
’s functional
equations.

Proposition 5
.

Let
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
.
Then,
X_{\mathbf{n}}
satisfies functional equations:

(2.29)

X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right),\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

where:

(2.30)

r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\binom{\mathbf{n}}{\mathbf{m}}a_{\mathbf{m},k}b_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m},k}

Using the
p
-adic shift operator, (
2.29
)
can be written as:

(2.31)

X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right),\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

Remark 16
.

Here,
\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m}
denotes the
d
-tuple
\left(n_{1}-m_{1},\ldots,n-m_{d}\right)
.

Proof: Letting
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
,
we have:

\displaystyle X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(X_{j}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)\right)^{n_{j}}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{use }(\ref{eq:X_j functional equations})\right);

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\left(a_{j,k}X_{j}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{j,k}\right)^{n_{j}}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{Binomial theorem}\right);

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{m=0}^{n_{j}}\binom{n_{j}}{m}a_{j,k}^{m}b_{j,k}^{n_{j}-m}X_{j}^{m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}\binom{\mathbf{n}}{\mathbf{m}}a_{\mathbf{m},k}b_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m},k}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Q.E.D.

Next, we investigate Fourier transforms. For this, we need some more
notation:

Definition 10
.

Given
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
,
we write
\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)

to denote the function:

(2.32)

\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt},\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}

We adopt a shorthand and write
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}
to denote
\alpha_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}}
,
and write
r_{\mathbf{n},k}
to denote
r_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n},k}

, so that:

(2.33)

r_{\mathbf{n},k}=\binom{\mathbf{n}}{\mathbf{n}}a_{\mathbf{n},k}b_{\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{n},k}=a_{\mathbf{n},k}=\prod_{j=1}^{d}a_{j,k}^{n_{j}}

and:

(2.34)

\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}r_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}

Remark 17
.

\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}
takes values in
\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
,
rather than
\mathcal{A}
because of those roots of unity given by
the
e^{-2\pi ikt}
s.

Example 2
.

Much of what we will do is motivated
by two desires: to make formal computations rigorous, and to understand
how to compensate for when those formal computations break down.

Proceeding formally, let’s suppose that, for all
\mathbf{n}
, we
can realize
X_{\mathbf{n}}
as a measure
X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}
.
Its Fourier-Stieltjes transform would then be given by:

(2.35)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{-2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}

Using the change of variable formula for
p
-adic integration:

(2.36)

\int_{\mathfrak{a}\mathbb{Z}_{p}+\mathfrak{b}}f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}=\left|\mathfrak{a}\right|_{p}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}f\left(\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{z}+\mathfrak{b}\right)d\mathfrak{z}

for all
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
with
\mathfrak{a}\neq 0

and all nice
f
, along with the partition of unity:

(2.37)

1=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p}{\equiv}k\right],\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

and the fact that:

(2.38)

\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p}{\equiv}k\right]f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}=\int_{p\mathbb{Z}_{p}+k}f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}

and:

(2.39)

\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}d\mathfrak{z}=1

we have, for any
\mathbf{n}
:

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{-2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p}{\equiv}k\right]e^{-2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\int_{p\mathbb{Z}_{p}+k}e^{-2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}X_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{change of variable}\right);

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{-2\pi i\left\{t\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)\right\}_{p}}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)d\mathfrak{z}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{apply }(\ref{eq:Z functional equation})\right);

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{-2\pi i\left\{pt\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}e^{-2\pi ikt}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)d\mathfrak{z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{e}}\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}\right)}_{\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}e^{-2\pi i\left\{pt\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)d\mathfrak{z}}_{\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right)

This suggests the functional equation:

(2.40)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right)

ought to be a relation between the Fourier transforms of the
X_{\mathbf{n}}
.
Setting
t=0
, pulling out the
\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{n}
term gives
us:

(2.41)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)

If
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\neq 1
, we can then solve for

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)
in terms of
\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}
s
and
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}
for all
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
. Moreover,
given any
t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
,
(
2.40
) shows that the value
of
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}
at
t
depends on the values of
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}

at
pt
for all
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
. Since
\left|pt\right|_{p}=\left|t\right|_{p}/p
,
it then follows that we can recursively solve (
2.40
)
for
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)
for all
t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
.

This motivates two key questions:

Question 1
.

Fix
\mathbf{n}
, and suppose
the functions
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}
are known for all
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
,
and that they are Fourier transforms of
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}
in
the sense that:

(2.42)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Then, when does there exist
\hat{\chi}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)

satisfying:

(2.43)

\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{\chi}\left(pt\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right),\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\textrm{ ?}

Moreover, if
\hat{\chi}
exists, is it unique?

Question 2
.

Let everything be as given in

Question
1
. What, if any,
solutions
\hat{\chi}
of (
2.43
) are Fourier
transforms of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
in the sense that:

(2.44)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Proposition

6
,
given below, answers
Question
1

in full.

Proposition 6
.

If:

I.
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}
is given for all
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
;

II.
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\neq 1
;

then (
2.43
) has a unique solution
\hat{\chi}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)

given by:

(2.45)

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)}\prod_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

On the other hand, if
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)=1
, there
are two possibilities:

I.
\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\neq 0
,
in which case (
2.43
) has no solutions.

II.
\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)=0
,
in which case (
2.43
) has infinitely many solutions.
In particular, the set of
\hat{\chi}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)

satisfying (
2.43
) is a
1
-dimensional affine

K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
-vector subspace of the space of
functions
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
.

Proof: First, suppose
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\neq 1
. Then:

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overbrace{\hat{\chi}\left(pt\right)}^{\textrm{expand}}+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\left(\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(pt\right)\hat{\chi}\left(p^{2}t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(pt\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(p^{2}t\right)\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\chi}\left(p^{N}t\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

For
t\neq 0
, choose
N=-v_{p}\left(t\right)
, so that
p^{N}t\overset{1}{\equiv}0
.
Then:

(2.46)

\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)=\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)\prod_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

Using (
2.43
), since
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\neq 1
,
we can set
t=0
and solve for
\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)
directly
to obtain:

(2.47)

\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)=\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)}

Next, suppose
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)=1
. Then setting

t=0
in (
2.43
) gives us:

(2.48)

\cancel{\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)}=\cancel{\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)}+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right),\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\textrm{ }?

and so, we see that the vanishing of the sum on the right is a necessary
condition for a solution to exist. So, supposing the sum on the right
vanishes, fix a constant
c\in\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
,
and let us look for solutions
\hat{\chi}
with
\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)=c
.
Then, for
\left|t\right|_{p}=p
, (
2.43
) becomes:

(2.49)

\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)=c\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)

Since the
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}
s are given functions, this then
determines the values of
\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)
for all
\left|t\right|_{p}=p
.
Proceeding recursively, we can solve (
2.43
) for

\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)
for all
t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
.

Now, picking
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
,
let
\hat{\chi}^{\prime}
be the solution of (
2.43
)
constructed from the initial condition
\hat{\chi}^{\prime}\left(0\right)=c^{\prime}
.
Then, writing
\hat{\mu}\left(t\right)
to denote
\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)-\hat{\chi}^{\prime}\left(t\right)
,
the functional equations satisfied by
\hat{\chi}
and
\hat{\chi}^{\prime}

tell us that:

(2.50)

\hat{\mu}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{\mu}\left(pt\right),\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}

By induction, for all
t\neq 0
, it follows that:

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{\mu}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(pt\right)\hat{\mu}\left(p^{2}t\right)

\displaystyle\left(p^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)}t\overset{1}{\equiv}0\right);

\displaystyle=\hat{\mu}\left(0\right)\prod_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)

which shows that the difference of any two solutions of (
2.43
)
lie in the
1
-dimensional
K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
-vector
space generated by the function:

(2.51)

t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\mapsto\prod_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\in\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)

with the right-hand side being defined as
1
for
t=0
. This shows
that the solution space of (
2.43
) is a
1
-dimensional
affine subspace of the vector space of functions
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
.

Q.E.D.

Answering
Question
2
, on
the other hand, is significantly more involved.

Example 3
.

To start with, let’s consider the simplest
possible case, where
\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)=1
. That is,

X_{\mathbf{n}}
is just a single F-series, characterized by the
equations:

(2.52)

X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)=a_{\mathbf{n},k}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{\mathbf{n},k},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

The only tuple
\mathbf{m}
with
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
is the
zero tuple, where
X_{\mathbf{0}}
, recall, is defined as the constant
function
1
. As such:

(2.53)

r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}=\begin{cases}a_{\mathbf{n},k}&\textrm{if}\textrm{ }\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{n}\\
b_{\mathbf{n},k}&\textrm{if }\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{0}\end{cases}

Turning to (
2.43
), our candidate
\hat{\chi}

for
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}
must satisfy:

(2.54)

\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{\chi}\left(pt\right)+\alpha_{\mathbf{0}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}\left(pt\right)

Here, we have
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}\left(pt\right)=\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)
,
which vanishes when
\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}
. Meanwhile:

(2.55)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}r_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}a_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}

(2.56)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathbf{0}}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}r_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}b_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}

and so:

(2.57)

\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)=\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}a_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}\right)\hat{\chi}\left(pt\right)+\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}b_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}\right)\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)

When
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)=1
, setting
t=0
gives
us:

(2.58)

\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)=\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}a_{\mathbf{n},k}\right)}_{1}\hat{\chi}\left(0\right)+\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}b_{\mathbf{n},k}

So, there will be infinitely many solutions of (
2.43
)
if and only if:

(2.59)

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}b_{\mathbf{n},k}=0

and will be
no solutions
otherwise. However, the formula for

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}
given by
Theorem
2

works
even when
(
2.59
) fails to hold.

This shows that the answer to
Question
2

is, in general, a resounding
no
.

Thankfully, we can deal with both of these cases by using the same
general approach I took in my dissertation, described below.

Definition 11
.

Given a function
f
on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, and given an integer
N\geq 0
,
we write
f_{N}
to denote the
N
th truncation of
f
,
defined by:

(2.60)

f_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}f\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

As defined,
f_{N}
is locally constant mod
p^{N}
. Because of
this, the Fourier transform of
f_{N}
exists, and is given by:

(2.61)

\hat{f}_{N}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{N}t\right)}{p^{N}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N}-1}f\left(n\right)e^{-2\pi int}

where
t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
. Note that
\hat{f}_{N}\left(t\right)

then vanishes for all
\left|t\right|_{p}>p^{N}
.

If
f
itself has subscripts, the Fourier transform of
f
’s
N
th
truncation will be given by putting a hat on
f
and appending an

N
as the right-most subscript of
f
. For example:

(2.62)

\hat{f}_{p_{\sqrt{7}},\&^{2},N}\left(t\right)

denotes the Fourier transform of the
N
th truncation of the function

f_{p_{\sqrt{7}},\&^{2},N}
.

Remark 18
.

It is important to remember that even if
f
itself might not have
a well-defined Fourier transform, the locally constant function
f_{N}

obtained by truncating
f

always
has a Fourier transform.
So, even though we might not yet know whether or not
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}

exists, we can unambiguously write
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}
, because
this is the Fourier transform of the locally constant function
X_{\mathbf{n},N}
.

Proposition 7
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
For all
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
:

(2.63)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(pt\right),\textrm{ }\forall\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}

Furthermore:

(2.64)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1-n}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

for all
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
.

Proof: We apply the functional equations of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
to the
definition of
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}
. Letting
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}
,
we have:

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{N}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N}-1}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(n\right)e^{-2\pi int}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N-1}-1}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(pn+k\right)e^{-2\pi i\left(pn+k\right)t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{N}}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N-1}-1}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)e^{-2\pi i\left(pn+k\right)t}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}\underbrace{\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}}_{\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)}\underbrace{\frac{1}{p^{N-1}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N-1}-1}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)e^{-2\pi in\left(pt\right)}}_{\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(pt\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(pt\right)

Thus, in particular:

(2.65)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(pt\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(pt\right)

As such, we can use the left-hand side to substitute for
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(pt\right)

on the right-hand side:

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(pt\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(pt\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N-2}\left(p^{2}t\right)+\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(pt\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-2}\left(p^{2}t\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\cdots

Proceeding to the
N
th step, we see that the pattern is:

(2.66)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)=\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},0}\left(p^{N}t\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1-n}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

as desired. Finally, note that:

(2.67)

X_{\mathcal{\mathbf{n}},0}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=X_{\mathcal{\mathbf{n}}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{0}}\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)

is a constant. As such, its Fourier transform is:

(2.68)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},0}\left(t\right)=X_{\mathcal{\mathbf{n}}}\left(0\right)\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(t\right)

and so:

(2.69)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},0}\left(p^{N}t\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{N}t\right)

Since
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}
,
p^{N}t\overset{1}{\equiv}0
,
which means:

(2.70)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},0}\left(p^{N}t\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{N}t\right)=\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},0}\left(p^{N}t\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)

and so:

(2.71)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1-n}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

Q.E.D.

For reference, if we were to apply this procedure to a single
p
-adic
F-series
X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
characterized by the equations:

(2.72)

X\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)=a_{j}X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{j},\textrm{ }\forall j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

we would find that:

(2.73)

\hat{X}_{N}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{N-1}\left(pt\right)+\beta_{X}\left(t\right)\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)

for certain functions
\alpha_{X}
and
\beta_{X}
of
t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
.
Like with
\hat{X}_{3,N}
, this formula can be nested to obtain a
closed-form expression for
\hat{X}_{N}\left(t\right)
:

(2.74)

\hat{X}_{N}\left(t\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)\beta_{X}\left(p^{n}t\right)\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m}t\right),\textrm{ }\forall N\geq 1,\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}

Observe that since
\alpha_{X}
is a function on
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}=\mathbb{Z}\left[1/p\right]/\mathbb{Z}
,
it is
1
-periodic, and thus:

(2.75)

\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m}t\right)=\alpha_{X}\left(0\right),\textrm{ }\forall m\geq-v_{p}\left(t\right)

Consequently, for all
t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\backslash\left\{0\right\}

and all
n\geq 0
:

(2.76)

\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m}t\right)=\left(\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)^{\max\left\{n+v_{p}\left(t\right),0\right\}}\prod_{m=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m}t\right)

In
[
16
]
, I used this to derive an exact formula
for
\hat{X}_{N}\left(t\right)
for fixed
t
. This was of the form:

(2.77)

\hat{X}_{N}\left(t\right)=\hat{F}\left(t\right)+\hat{G}_{N}\left(t\right)

where
\hat{F},\hat{G}_{N}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}

were functions.
\hat{G}_{N}\left(t\right)
can be thought of as
the “divergent term” which failed to converge to a limit as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
while
\hat{F}\left(t\right)
was the fine structure hidden beneath
the divergent tumult. Miraculously, it turned out that the Fourier
series generated by
\hat{F}
converged pointwise everywhere to
X
:

(2.78)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{F}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}\overset{\mathcal{F}}{=}X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right),\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

albeit with the caveat that the limit had to be evaluated with respect
to some frame
\mathcal{F}
. The convergence of (
2.78
)
then justified calling
\hat{F}
the Fourier transform of
X
, and
denoting it by
\hat{X}
.

In summary, the method is to obtain an asymptotic decomposition of
the form (
2.77
), discard the divergent
terms, and then hope that leftovers generate a Fourier series that
sums to
X
. The most important step is the summation of the Fourier
series (
2.78
), where we verify that our candidate
for the Fourier transform of our function does, indeed, generate our
function’s Fourier series.

So, beginning with the formula:

(2.79)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(t\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1-n}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

obtained in
Proposition
7
,
we make the following educated guess as to what the Fourier transform
of
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}
might be.

Definition 12
.

Now, for all
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
,
set:

(2.80)

\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-2}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

with
n
-sum being defined as
0
when
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p
.
Then, let:

(2.81)

\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)

\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}
is going to be our guess for the Fourier transform
of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
. To see how accurate the guess is, we’ll need
to sum the Fourier series it generates, which we denote by:

(2.82)

\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{m},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

Remark 19
.

I would like to be able to explain rigorously why these completely
ignore the
X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)

term of (
2.79
), but I can’t, so I won’t. Suffice
it to say, we’ll deal with that term in a bit.

Remark 20
.

The attentive reader will note that this guess does not agree with
the solution of
Proposition
6

in the case where
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\neq 1
. We will
discuss this soon enough.

Computing
\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{m},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
will
be relatively easy, thanks to a Fourier transform identity given below.
Before we can state and prove it, however, we need an auxiliary identity:

For this, we will need the following summation identity:

Proposition 8
.

Let
A
be an abelian group, let
\hat{f},\hat{g}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow A
,
and let
r\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
. Then, for any
N\geq r
:

(2.83)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}\left(t\right)\hat{g}\left(p^{r}t\right)=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N-r}}\sum_{k=0}^{p^{r}-1}\hat{f}\left(\frac{t+k}{p^{r}}\right)\hat{g}\left(t\right)=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N-r}}\sum_{\left|s\right|_{p}\leq p^{r}}\hat{f}\left(\frac{t}{p^{r}}+s\right)\hat{g}\left(t\right)

where the operator
\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N-r}}
is evaluation
at
t=0
in the case where
N=r
.

Proof: Direct computation.

Q.E.D.

The main Fourier transform identity we need is:

Proposition 9
.

Let
\hat{\chi}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)
.
Then, for any constants
q_{0},\ldots,q_{p-1}
, all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
and all
N\geq n
:

(2.84)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\hat{\chi}\left(p^{n}t\right)\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijp^{k}t}\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\right)\tilde{\chi}_{N-n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right),\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

where:

(2.85)

\tilde{\chi}_{L}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{L}}\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

In particular, this shows that
\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\right)\tilde{\chi}_{L}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

has:

(2.86)

\hat{\chi}\left(p^{n}t\right)\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijp^{k}t}\right)

as a Fourier transform.

Proof:

Claim 1
.

Let
g:\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow K
be locally constant. Then, we
have the Fourier transform pair:

(2.87)

q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)\sim\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}\right)\hat{g}\left(pt\right)

Proof of claim: Let
g
be locally constant mod
p^{N_{g}}
for
some
N_{g}\geq 0
. Then, note that for
n\geq 0
:

(2.88)

f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

is locally constant as well. In particular, letting
n\geq 1
and

\mathfrak{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
be arbitrary:

\displaystyle f\left(\mathfrak{z}+p^{n}\mathfrak{y}\right)

\displaystyle=q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}+p^{n}\mathfrak{y}\right]_{p}}g\left(\frac{\mathfrak{z}+p^{n}\mathfrak{y}-\left[\mathfrak{z}+p^{n}\mathfrak{y}\right]_{p}}{p}\right)

\displaystyle=q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g\left(\frac{\mathfrak{z}-\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}{p}+p^{n-1}\mathfrak{y}\right)

\displaystyle=q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+p^{n-1}\mathfrak{y}\right)

So, choosing
n=N_{g}+1
, we get:

\displaystyle f\left(\mathfrak{z}+p^{N_{g}+1}\mathfrak{y}\right)

\displaystyle=q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+p^{N_{g}}\mathfrak{y}\right)

\displaystyle\left(g\textrm{ is loc. const. mod }p^{N_{g}}\right);

\displaystyle=q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Thus, we see that
f
is locally constant mod
p^{N_{g}+1}
. As
such,
f
’s Fourier transform is given by:

\displaystyle\hat{f}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{N_{g}+1}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N_{g}+1}-1}f\left(n\right)e^{-2\pi itn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{N_{g}+1}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N_{g}+1}-1}q_{\left[n\right]_{p}}g\left(\theta_{p}\left(n\right)\right)e^{-2\pi itn}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{split sum mod }n\right);

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{N_{g}+1}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N_{g}}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{\left[pn+j\right]_{p}}g\left(\theta_{p}\left(pn+j\right)\right)e^{-2\pi it\left(pn+j\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p^{N_{g}+1}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N_{g}}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{\left[j\right]_{p}}g\left(n\right)e^{-2\pi it\left(pn+j\right)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}\right)\underbrace{\frac{1}{p^{N_{g}}}\sum_{n=0}^{p^{N_{g}}-1}g\left(n\right)e^{-2\pi iptn}}_{\hat{g}\left(pt\right),\textrm{ since }g\textrm{ is loc. const. mod }p^{N_{g}}}

This proves the claim.
\checkmark

So, letting
\chi
be quasi-integrable, observe that
\tilde{\chi}_{L}

is then a locally constant function, and
\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{L}t\right)\hat{\chi}\left(t\right)

is its Fourier transform. Hence, the above gives:

(2.89)

\sum_{t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\left(\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{L+1}t\right)\hat{\chi}\left(pt\right)\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\underbrace{q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{\chi}_{L}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)}_{g_{1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)},\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

which holds for all
L
.

Next, fixing
L
, let
g_{n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=q_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\tilde{\chi}_{L}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)
.
Since
g_{1}
is locally constant, we have the Fourier transform
pair:

\displaystyle q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g_{1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\sim\hat{g}_{1}\left(pt\right)\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{L+2}t\right)\hat{\chi}\left(p^{2}t\right)\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijpt}\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{L+2}t\right)\hat{\chi}\left(p^{2}t\right)\prod_{k=0}^{2-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijp^{k}t}\right)

Since:

(2.90)

q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}g_{1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)=q_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}q_{\left[\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\tilde{\chi}_{L}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ 2}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

by induction, we have the Fourier transform pair:

(2.91)

\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\right)\tilde{\chi}_{L}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)\sim\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{L+n}t\right)\hat{\chi}\left(p^{n}t\right)\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijp^{k}t}\right)

and hence:

(2.92)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{L+n}}\left(\hat{\chi}\left(p^{n}t\right)\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}q_{j}e^{-2\pi ijp^{k}t}\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\right)\tilde{\chi}_{L}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right),\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

Finally, letting
N\geq n
, set
L=N-n
.

Q.E.D.

Using this, we can compute the Fourier series generated
by the
\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m}}
s.

Notation 2
.

For all
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
,
define
\kappa_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\textrm{Frac}\mathcal{A}

be defined by:

(2.93)

\kappa_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(n\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}}{r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},0}}\right)^{\#_{p:k}\left(n\right)}

and let
\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}
denote
\kappa_{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}}
.
Also, write:

(2.94)

\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right)

We break the computation into several steps:

Proposition 10
.

For all
N\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
,
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
,
and
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
:

(2.95)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

where:

(2.96)

\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N-n-1}}\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{y}\right\}_{p}},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

Proof: By
Proposition
9
,
we can write:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\right)\tilde{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},N-n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{Use {Proposition} {\ref{prop:Product identity for kappa_X}}}\right);

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\tilde{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},N-n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

Next, letting
\mathfrak{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we have:

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},N-n}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N-n}}\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{y}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N-n}}\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{y}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{Use {Proposition} {\ref{prop:Fourier transforms and shifts}}}\right);

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{y}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)\right)

Finally, set
\mathfrak{y}=\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
.

Q.E.D.

Proposition 11
.

For all
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}

with
\mathbf{m}\leq\mathbf{n}
, and for all
N\geq 2
, we have:

(2.97)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

for all
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, so that:

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

(2.98)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Proof: Since
\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)
vanishes
for
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p
, we can write:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p^{2}\leq\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\sum_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-2}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=2}^{N}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p^{h}}\sum_{n=0}^{h-2}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{re-arrange $\sum$s}\right);

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}\sum_{h=n+2}^{N}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p^{h}}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}\sum_{p^{n+2}\leq\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

Here, we write the
t
-sum on the bottom line as the difference:

(2.99)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}-\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{n+1}}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\hat{Y}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

Applying
Proposition

10

to this yields:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},0}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)\right)

Finally, noting:

(2.100)

\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},0}\left(\mathfrak{y}\right)=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq 0}\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{y}\right\}_{p}}=\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right),\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

we can then write:

(2.101)

\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Q.E.D.

So, summing over all
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
, we have:

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Now, we skip over one of the biggest details:

Assumption 4
.

For all
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
:

(2.102)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

is equal to:

(2.103)

f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Hence, setting:

(2.104)

f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

we have that:

(2.105)

f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Remark 21
.

In this, note that we are avoiding any discussion of either the mode
of convergence and the set of
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for
which the convergence occurs. Furthermore, we are assuming the quasi-integrability
of
X_{\mathbf{m}}
for all
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
. These details
will be dealt with in
Sections
3

and
4
. The notion of frames
presented in
Section
3.1
was
originally tailor-made to give a rigorous foundation to the notion
of convergence occurring in (
2.102
). The nature of
quasi-integrability, meanwhile, is chronicled in
Section
3.3
.
Moreover, while the work of
Section
3

will give us the language needed to
define
the sense in which
(
2.102
) converges, we will still need to formulate
an argument to justify that the limit in (
2.102
) actually
converges in the sense defined in
Section
3
.
This argument, as well as the terminology and preparatory work needed
to see it through, are covered in
Section
4
,
with
Lemma
3
on page
3

giving sufficient conditions for which a general form of the limit
in (
2.102
) holds in the sense of
Section
3.1
.

Lemma 1
.

If
Assumption
4

holds,
f_{\mathbf{n}}
is quasi-integrable, with:

(2.106)

\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\hat{f}_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)

as a Fourier transform.

Provided everything up to this point is justified, this will be enough
to prove the quasi-integrability of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
.

Theorem 3
.

X_{\mathbf{n}}

is quasi-integrable. In particular, by recursion-induction, for all

\mathbf{n}
with
\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)\geq 2
, we have:

(2.107)

\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)=\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)-\hat{g}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)

where:

(2.108)

\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\sum_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-2}\left(\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{m}t\right)\right)\alpha_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(p^{n+1}t\right)

and where:

(2.109)

\hat{g}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if }t=0\\
\left(\beta_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)v_{p}\left(t\right)+\gamma_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\frac{t\left|t\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\right)\hat{A}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }t\neq 0\end{cases},\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}

if
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)=1
and:

(2.110)

\hat{g}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)=\begin{cases}\frac{\beta_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)}&\textrm{if }t=0\\
\left(\frac{\beta_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)}+\gamma_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\frac{t\left|t\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\right)\hat{A}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }t\neq 0\end{cases},\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}

if
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)\neq 1
, where
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}},\beta_{\mathbf{n}},\gamma_{\mathbf{n}},\hat{A}_{\mathbf{n}}:\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\rightarrow\textrm{Frac}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\otimes_{K}K\left(\zeta_{p^{\infty}}\right)

are given by:

(2.111)

\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}r_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}

(2.112)

\beta_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}c_{\mathbf{n},k}e^{-2\pi ikt}

(2.113)

\gamma_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{\beta_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)}

(2.114)

\hat{A}_{\mathbf{n}}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{n=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p^{n}t\right)

where:

(2.115)

c_{\mathbf{n},k}=-\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},k}\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)

Proof: Let
\mathfrak{z}
and
\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}
be arbitrary,
let
k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
and pull out the
n=0
term
of
f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}
(note:
\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{0}}\right)=\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(0\right)=1
).
This gives:

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[p\mathfrak{z}+k\right]_{p^{n}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)\right]_{p}}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{Proposition}\textrm{ }\mathbf{\ref{prop:fundamental functional equations-1}}\right);

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{r_{\mathbf{n},0}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(k\right)}_{r_{\mathbf{n},k}}\underbrace{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n-1}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n-1}}\right)r_{\mathbf{m},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n-1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)}_{f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)+r_{\mathbf{n},k}f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}f_{\mathbf{m}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}\left(r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)+r_{\mathbf{n},k}f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)+r_{\mathbf{n},k}\underbrace{\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}f_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}_{f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}

Using
Proposition
5
, we have:

(2.116)

\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)-r_{\mathbf{n},k}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Thus:

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)+r_{\mathbf{n},k}f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)-r_{\mathbf{n},k}X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},k}\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)+r_{\mathbf{n},k}f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)-X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},k}\left(f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},k}\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)

Now, set
g_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
,
and let:

(2.117)

c_{\mathbf{n},k}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}-\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},k}\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right),\textrm{ }\forall k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

Then, we have:

(2.118)

g_{\mathbf{n}}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)=r_{\mathbf{n},k}g\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+c_{\mathbf{n},k},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z},\textrm{ }\forall k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

Ah, but this shows that
g_{\mathbf{n}}
is a foliated F-series,
and we already know how to compute the Fourier transform of such a
function by using the formulae from
Theorem
2
.
This shows that
g_{\mathbf{n}}
is quasi-integrable. Finally, since

X_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-g_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
,
we see that
\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}-\hat{g}_{\mathbf{n}}
is then a
Fourier transform of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
, which proves the quasi-integrability
of
X_{\mathbf{n}}
, and gives us a formula for it, as well!

Q.E.D.

Note 4
.

There are frame-theoretic concerns that need to be addressed here.
Fortunately, these are already ones that we’ve set aside for discussion
later on.

What remains is to deal with the inductive-recursive aspect of our
argument. Proving the quasi-integrability of a degree
d
F-series
will require leveraging the quasi-integrability of F-series of degrees

0
through
d-1
. The actual balancing point is in
Assumption

4
. Proving that this assumption is justified
requires knowing an
explicit
upper bound on the difference:

(2.119)

\Delta_{N}^{\left(n\right)}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\left(\tilde{X}_{N}\circ\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\right)\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\left(X\circ\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\right)\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

(2.120)

\Delta_{\mathbf{m},N}^{\left(0\right)}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

between
X_{\mathbf{m}}
and the
N
th partial sum of
X_{\mathbf{m}}
’s
Fourier series. Obtaining this bound is a tedious but ultimately straightforward
matter of computing
\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
.
This is quite nice, because it tells us that we can deal with all
of the necessary computations in one fell swoop.

We’ll deal with
\hat{g}_{\mathbf{n}}
first. This is a simple matter
of computing (
2.120
) when
\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)=1
.

Proposition 12
.

Set:

(2.121)

\kappa_{X}\left(m\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}

Then:

(2.122)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

for all
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Next, letting:

(2.123)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}a_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}

(2.124)

\beta_{X}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}b_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}

(2.125)

\gamma_{X}\left(t\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{\beta_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}

we have:

(2.126)

\sum_{0<\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{t\left|t\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\beta_{X}\left(\frac{j}{p}\right)\varepsilon_{n}^{j}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

Proof: First, we write:

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}a_{j}e^{-2\pi ijp^{m}t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\right)^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)}\prod_{m=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^{p-1}\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}e^{-2\pi ijp^{m}t}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}\left(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\right)^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)}\sum_{m=0}^{\left|t\right|_{p}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)e^{-2\pi imt}

The step marked (!) is an elementary digit-counting combinatorial
argument. Consequently:

\displaystyle\sum_{0<\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p^{n}}\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p^{n}}\left(\left(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\right)^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)}\sum_{m=0}^{\left|t\right|_{p}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)e^{-2\pi imt}\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\right)^{n}\sum_{m=0}^{p^{n}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p^{n}}e^{2\pi i\left\{t\left(\mathfrak{z}-m\right)\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\right)^{n}\sum_{m=0}^{p^{n}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)\left(p^{n}\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p^{n}}{\equiv}m\right]-p^{n-1}\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p^{n-1}}{\equiv}m\right]\right)

Here, we have:

(2.127)

\sum_{m=0}^{p^{n}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p^{n}}{\equiv}m\right]=\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}=\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

while:

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{p^{n}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p^{n-1}}{\equiv}m\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\sum_{m=kp^{n-1}}^{\left(k+1\right)p^{n-1}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p^{n-1}}{\equiv}m\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\sum_{m=0}^{p^{n-1}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:j}\left(m+kp^{n-1}\right)}\right)\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p^{n-1}}{\equiv}m+kp^{n-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\sum_{m=0}^{p^{n-1}-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\left[j=k\right]+\#_{p:j}\left(m\right)}\right)\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p^{n-1}}{\equiv}m\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\prod_{j=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{j}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\left[j=k\right]+\#_{p:j}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n-1}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a_{0}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}a_{k}\right)\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n-1}}\right)

and so:

\displaystyle\sum_{0<\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(\left(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\right)^{n}p^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)-\left(\frac{a_{0}}{p}\right)^{n}p^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{a_{0}}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}a_{k}\right)\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n-1}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)-\sum_{n=1}^{N}a_{0}^{n-1}\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}a_{k}\right)}_{\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{0}}\right)=1\right);

\displaystyle=a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)+\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

Thus:

(2.128)

\sum_{0<\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)+\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

Since
\hat{A}_{X}\left(0\right)=1
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)+\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

The proof of (
2.126
) is similar, only more involved.
One must deal with it by writing:

(2.129)

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{t\left|t\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p^{N}-1}\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{k}{p}\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(\frac{k}{p^{N}}\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{k\mathfrak{z}/p^{N}\right\}_{p}}

and then using the sum decomposition formula:

(2.130)

\sum_{k=0}^{p^{N}-1}f\left(k\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{p^{N-1}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}f\left(pk+j\right)

[
16
]
contains the proof in
Lemma 4.4
of
Section 4, on page 259.

Q.E.D.

Proposition 13
.
