Given a
p
-adic F-series
X
characterized by:

(2.131)

X\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)=a_{j}X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{j},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

that is compatible with a frame
\mathcal{F}
, let
\hat{X}
be
as given by
Theorem
2
, though
without assuming that said theorem is true.

Under these conditions, we have that:

(2.132)

\hat{A}_{X}\left(pt\right)=\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(0\right)+\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\right)\frac{\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}

and:

(2.133)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)+\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\right)\hat{X}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1\\
\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\right)\left(\hat{X}\left(t\right)+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)\right)&\textrm{if }\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\end{cases},\textrm{ }\forall t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}

Consequently:

(2.134)

\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}-\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]\beta_{X}\left(0\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

for all
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof: The proof of (
2.132
) follows by simple
re-indexing. When
\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}
:

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{X}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{-v_{p}\left(pt\right)-1}\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m}pt\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-2}\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m+1}t\right)

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{re-index}\right);

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=1}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m}t\right)

\displaystyle\left(\times\frac{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}\underbrace{\prod_{m=0}^{-v_{p}\left(t\right)-1}\alpha_{X}\left(p^{m}t\right)}_{\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)}

When
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p
, on the other hand,
pt
is an integer,
and so, the
1
-periodicity of
\hat{A}_{X}
forces
\hat{A}_{X}\left(pt\right)=\hat{A}_{X}\left(0\right)=1
.
Thus:

(2.135)

\hat{A}_{X}\left(pt\right)=\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(0\right)+\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\right)\frac{\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}

where
\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)
is
1
if
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p

and is
0
otherwise.

Using this, the proof of (
2.133
) follows from
replacing
t
in
Theorem
2
’s
formulae for
\hat{X}
with
pt
and then applying (
2.132
).
We have two cases, based on whether or not
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)

is equal to
1
.

I. Suppose
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1
. Since
\left|pt\right|_{p}=0

all
\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{X}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\left(\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}+\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{pt\left|pt\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(pt\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{Use }(\ref{eq:A_X-hat recurrence})\right);

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\left(\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}+\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{t\left|t\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\right)\frac{\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\hat{X}\left(0\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\frac{\hat{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}\end{cases}

Multiplying through by
\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)
gives us:

(2.136)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\begin{cases}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\hat{X}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}\end{cases}=\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)+\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\right)\hat{X}\left(t\right)

as desired.

II. Suppose
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1
. Then, since

v_{p}\left(pt\right)=v_{p}\left(t\right)+1
:

\displaystyle\hat{X}\left(pt\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\left(\beta_{X}\left(0\right)v_{p}\left(pt\right)+\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{pt\left|pt\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(pt\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{Use }(\ref{eq:A_X-hat recurrence})\right);

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\frac{\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}+\underbrace{\left(\beta_{X}\left(0\right)v_{p}\left(t\right)+\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{t\left|t\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\right)\frac{\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}}_{\hat{X}\left(t\right)/\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)+\hat{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\geq p^{2}\end{cases}

Multiplying through by
\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)
gives the desired
result:

(2.137)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\left(1-\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)\right)\left(\hat{X}\left(t\right)+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)\right)

Finally, we take (
2.133
) and sum the generated
Fourier series. This necessitates two cases, based on whether or not

\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1
.

I. When
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1
and
N\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\hat{X}\left(0\right)\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}+\sum_{p^{2}\leq\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\hat{X}\left(0\right)\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}+\underbrace{\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}}_{\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p}\hat{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p}\left(\hat{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)-\hat{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

Here:

(2.138)

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p}\left(\hat{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)-\hat{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\hat{X}\left(0\right)\left(\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)-1\right)+\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\left(\hat{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)-\hat{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

Since
\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)
for
\left|t\right|_{p}=p
,
using
2
’s formula for
\hat{X}
,
and noting that
t\left|t\right|_{p}/p=t
when
\left|t\right|_{p}
,
we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\left(\hat{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)-\hat{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\left(\hat{X}\left(0\right)-\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}-\gamma_{X}\left(t\right)\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle\left(\gamma_{X}\left(t\right)=\frac{\beta_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}\right);

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\left(\underbrace{\hat{X}\left(0\right)-\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}}_{0}-\frac{\beta_{X}\left(t\right)}{\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)}\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\beta_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}b_{j}\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}e^{2\pi i\left\{t\left(\mathfrak{z}-j\right)\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}b_{j}\left(p\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p}{\equiv}j\right]-1\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}b_{j}\left[\mathfrak{z}\overset{p}{\equiv}j\right]+\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}b_{j}

\displaystyle=-b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)

and so:

(2.139)

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\hat{X}\left(0\right)\left(\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)-1\right)-b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)

Here, we note that:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}a_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}a_{j}e^{-2\pi ijt}\right)\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{N}t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{Use {Proposition }{\ref{prop:Fourier transforms and shifts}}}\right);

\displaystyle=a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\sum_{t\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(p^{N-1}t\right)\hat{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

Hence, (
2.139
) is:

\displaystyle a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\hat{X}\left(0\right)\left(\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)-1\right)-b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)

\displaystyle\left(\hat{X}\left(0\right)=\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}=\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}\right);

\displaystyle=\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)-b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}

Adding
b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}
to both sides gives the
desired result.

II. When
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1
:

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\sum_{p^{2}\leq\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\hat{X}\left(t\right)+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

As we already know what the left-hand side is, we just need to deal
with the right-hand side. For this,
Proposition
12

gives us:

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left(a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\underbrace{\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}_{0,\textrm{ since }\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1}\right)

while:

(2.140)

\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p}\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left(a_{0}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}\right)+\underbrace{\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)}_{0,\textrm{ since }\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1}\right)

Subtracting these two gives:

(2.141)

\sum_{p^{2}\leq\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left(a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)-a_{0}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}\right)\right)

Meanwhile:

(2.142)

\displaystyle\sum_{p^{2}\leq\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\hat{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\tilde{X}_{1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Hence:

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p^{2}\leq\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p^{N}}\left(\hat{X}\left(t\right)+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left(a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)-a_{0}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}\right)\right)+\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\tilde{X}_{1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Here, we note that:

(2.143)

\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}\right)=\frac{a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}}{a_{0}}

which leaves us with:

(2.144)

a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left(a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)-a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\right)+\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\tilde{X}_{1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Finally:

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\overbrace{\hat{X}\left(0\right)}^{0}+\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\hat{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\left(\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\overbrace{v_{p}\left(t\right)}^{-1}+\gamma_{X}\left(\frac{t\left|t\right|_{p}}{p}\right)\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle\left(\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{X}\left(t\right),\textrm{ }\forall\left|t\right|_{p}=p\right);

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\left(\gamma_{X}\left(t\right)-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}=p}\left(\beta_{X}\left(t\right)-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle=-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\overbrace{\left(1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\right)}^{0}+\sum_{\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p}\left(\beta_{X}\left(t\right)-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\right)e^{2\pi i\left\{t\mathfrak{z}\right\}_{p}}

\displaystyle\left(\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right);

\displaystyle=b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}

Hence (
2.144
) becomes:

\displaystyle a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left(a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)-a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\right)+\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\left(b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}

and so:

(2.145)

\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

which is (
2.134
).

Q.E.D.

Proposition 14
.

Letting everything be as given in

Proposition
13
, we have:

(2.146)

\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\left(\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]N\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

where:

(2.147)

\Delta_{N}^{\left(n\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}X\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

for all
n,N\geq 0
.

Proof: We subtract
X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
from both sides of
equation (
2.134
)
Proposition

13
, and then use the identity:

(2.148)

X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}X\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+b_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}

from
Proposition
2.31
to get:

\underbrace{X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\tilde{X}_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}_{\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}=a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\underbrace{\left(X\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\tilde{X}_{N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)\right)}_{\Delta_{N-1}^{\left(1\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

which is:

(2.149)

\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=a_{\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1}^{\left(1\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+h_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

where:

(2.150)

h_{N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

Now, by induction on (
2.149
), we have:

(2.151)

\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}a_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}h_{N-n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)\prod_{m=0}^{n-1}a_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}

By
Proposition
2
,
we can write the products in (
2.151
) as:

(2.152)

\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}h_{N-n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

Here, applying
Proposition
4
,
we have that:

\displaystyle h_{N-n}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]a_{0}^{N-n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p^{N-n}}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p^{k}}\right)=\frac{\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{k+n}}\right)}{\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}\right);

\displaystyle=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]a_{0}^{N-n}\frac{\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)}{\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]\frac{a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)}{a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}

and so, (
2.152
) becomes:

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]\frac{a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)}{a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)}a_{0}^{n}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]N\beta_{X}\left(0\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{X\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\left[\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\right]N\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa_{X}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)

Q.E.D.

Next, we deal with
\hat{f}_{\mathbf{n}}
and all of

X_{\mathbf{n}}
.

Proposition 15
.

For all
N\geq 1
and all
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
:

(2.153)

\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Hence, if
Assumption
4
holds:

(2.154)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{N}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1-n}^{\left(n+1\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Proof: By
Proposition
11
,
we have the formula:

(2.155)

\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{N-2}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Direct computation shows:

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},k}\underbrace{\sum_{n=1}^{N-3}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1-n-1}\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ n+1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)}_{\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[k\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

and hence:

(2.156)

\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

which proves (
2.153
).

Assuming
Assumption
4
, we can
take
N\rightarrow\infty
and get:

(2.157)

f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)

Subtracting this from (
2.153
) gives:

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)-\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle-\left(r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\left(\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\hat{X}_{\mathbf{m}}\left(0\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\left(f_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\tilde{f}_{\mathbf{n},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\left(X_{\mathbf{m}}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)-\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{m},N-1}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1}^{\left(1\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1}^{\left(1\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

So, by induction:

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1}^{\left(1\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1}^{\left(1\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{iterate once}\right);

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-2}^{\left(2\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle+r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-2}^{\left(2\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1}^{\left(1\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\textrm{iterate }M\textrm{ times}\right);

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N-M}^{\left(M\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\prod_{m=0}^{M-1}r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\left(\prod_{n=0}^{m-1}r_{\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1-m}^{\left(m+1\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Using
Proposition
2
,
we can simplify this to:

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N-M}^{\left(M\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{M}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{M}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{m}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{m}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ m}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1-m}^{\left(m+1\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Setting
M=N
gives:

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{\left(0\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle=r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{N}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)\Delta_{0}^{\left(N\right)}\left\{f_{\mathbf{n}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{\mathbf{n},0}^{n}\kappa_{\mathbf{n}}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{m}<\mathbf{n}}r_{\mathbf{m},\mathbf{n},\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}\Delta_{N-1-n}^{\left(n+1\right)}\left\{X_{\mathbf{m}}\right\}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

Q.E.D.

Remark 22
.

Using the symbology of
Proposition

13
, the
\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)=1
case of the
functional equation (
2.43
) dealt with in
Example

3
can be written as:

(2.158)

\hat{X}\left(t\right)=\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)+\beta_{X}\left(t\right)\mathbf{1}_{0}\left(pt\right)

where
\hat{X}
is used in place of
\hat{X}_{\mathbf{n}}
, where

X_{\mathbf{0}}=\mathbf{1}_{0}
, where
\alpha_{X}=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}

and
\beta_{X}=\alpha_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{n}}
. Solving for
\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)

gives:

(2.159)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\begin{cases}\hat{X}\left(t\right)-\beta_{X}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\hat{X}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}>p\end{cases}

Meanwhile, the result of
Proposition
2

is:

(2.160)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\begin{cases}\begin{cases}\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\hat{X}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}>p\end{cases}&\textrm{if }\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1\\
\\
\begin{cases}0&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}\leq p\\
\hat{X}\left(t\right)+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)&\textrm{if }\left|t\right|_{p}>p\end{cases}&\textrm{if }\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1\end{cases}

We will call (
2.160
) the
corrected
equation
, while (
2.159
)
will be called the
uncorrected equation
.

Thus, we
have the following analysis:

•Suppose
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1
. When
t=0
,
the uncorrected equation is:

(2.161)

\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)=\hat{X}\left(0\right)-\beta_{X}\left(0\right)

Hence:

(2.162)

\frac{\beta_{X}\left(0\right)}{1-\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)}=X\left(0\right)

On the other hand, for
t=0
, the corrected equation is
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)=\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)
,
which is a tautology. Thus, the two equations are
not in conflict

for
t=0
. That is, solutions of one are not automatically barred
from being solutions of the other.

When
\left|t\right|_{p}=p
, the uncorrected equation is:

(2.163)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)=\hat{X}\left(t\right)-\beta_{X}\left(t\right)

while the corrected equation is once again a tautology.

(2.164)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)=\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(0\right)

Thus, the two equations
not in conflict
for
\left|t\right|_{p}=p
,
either.

Finally, when
\left|t\right|_{p}>p
, both equations are identical:

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\hat{X}\left(t\right)
.

Consequently,
the corrected and uncorrected equations are not
in conflict with one another when
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1
.

In particular, this shows that, in the
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1

case,
Theorem
2
’s formula
for
\hat{X}
is precisely the unique solution of the uncorrected
equation (
2.158
).
As such, the formal procedure for computing
\hat{X}
described in

Example
2
is justified in the
case where
\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)=1
and
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1
.

•Suppose
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1
. When
\left|t\right|_{p}>p
,
the uncorrected equation is:

(2.165)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\hat{X}\left(t\right)

while the corrected equation is:

(2.166)

\alpha_{X}\left(t\right)\hat{X}\left(pt\right)=\hat{X}\left(t\right)+\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\hat{A}_{X}\left(t\right)

Since
\hat{A}_{X}
is not identically
0
, these two are consistent
if and only if
\beta_{X}\left(0\right)=0
. When
\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 0
,
on the other hand, the corrected equation will have solutions, while—as
we saw in
Proposition
6
,
the uncorrected equation will have no solutions!

When
t=0
, the uncorrected equation forces
\beta_{X}\left(0\right)=0
,
while the corrected one forces
\hat{X}\left(0\right)=0
and leaves

\beta_{X}\left(0\right)
unaffected. On the other hand, as we saw
in
Proposition
6
, when

\beta_{X}\left(0\right)=0
, the uncorrected equation has infinitely
many solutions, each being uniquely determined by the initial value

\hat{X}\left(0\right)
, which may be chosen freely.

This gives the complete answer to
Question
2

in the case where
\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)=1
:

I. If
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 1
, then (
2.158
)
(our simplified version of equation (
2.43
) for
the
\Sigma\left(\mathbf{n}\right)=1
case) has a unique solution,
and that solution is a Fourier transform of
X
.

II. If
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1
and
\beta_{X}\left(0\right)=0
,
of the infinitely many solutions that (
2.158
)
possesses, only the one with the initial condition
\hat{X}\left(0\right)=0

is a Fourier transform of
X
.

III. If
\alpha_{X}\left(0\right)=1
and
\beta_{X}\left(0\right)\neq 0
,
(
2.158
) has
no solutions, and all of these non-existent solutions are not Fourier
transform of
X
.

3.
The Smaller Ideas

With the main computation out of the way, we can now turn to introducing
the concepts and set-up needed to rigorously justify our computations.

3.1.
Frame Theory

Intuitively, a frame device for recording unusual recipes for interpreting
sequences of functions as “convergent”. The following is an archetypical
example of the situation that motivated them.

Example 4
.

Let
p,q,d\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0,1,-1\right\}
,
where
p
and
q
are distinct primes and
d
is co-prime to both

p
and
q
. Then, consider the
3
-adic F-series:

(3.1)

X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{p^{\#_{3:1}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{3^{n}}\right)}q^{\#_{3:2}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{3^{n}}\right)}}{d^{n}}

Observe that for all non-negative integer values of
\mathfrak{z}
,

X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
converges in
\mathbb{R}
to a rational
number. Meanwhile, for any
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{3}\backslash\left\{0,1,2,\ldots\right\}
,
the series will converge:

•
p
-adically if
\mathfrak{z}
has infinitely many

1
s digits;

•
q
-adically if
\mathfrak{z}
has infinitely many

2
s digits;

•both
p
-adically
and

q
-adically if
\mathfrak{z}

has infinitely many
1
s and
2
s digits.

•
\infty
-adically, depending on the value of
\mathfrak{z}
.
Indeed, by the
root test
, we have that:

(3.2)

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{\#_{3:1}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{3^{n}}\right)}{n}\frac{\ln\left|p\right|}{\ln\left|d\right|}+\frac{\#_{3:2}\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{3^{n}}\right)}{n}\frac{\ln\left|q\right|}{\ln\left|d\right|}\right)<1

is a sufficient, though not necessary, condition for
X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

to converge in
\mathbb{R}
.

Thus, for example, we could realize
X
as a function
\mathbb{Z}_{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}\cup\mathbb{Q}_{q}\cup\mathbb{Q}

by defining the sum of (
3.1
) using the real topology
if
\mathfrak{z}\in\left\{0,1,2,\ldots\right\}
, using the
p
-adic
topology if
\mathfrak{z}
has infinitely many
1
s digits but finitely
many
2
s digits, and using the
q
-adic topology if
\mathfrak{z}

has infinitely many
2
s digits. This recipe for summing (
3.1
)
is an example of a frame. Obviously, there are infinitely many possible
choices of a frame that makes
X
summable at every
3
-adic integer

\mathfrak{z}
, and the flexibility of these choices encodes arithmetic
information about (
3.1
), despite their seemingly
ad-hoc nature. Indeed, as we will see, the everything that we will
do with F-series will work for any choice of frame satisfying the
necessary regularity hypotheses.

As the theory of frames is in its infancy, there is still much to
be done to develop it. One long term goal is to remove the ad-hoc
aspects of the construction and turn it into a means of capturing
arithmetic data.

To define frames in general, we need to use norms and seminorms on
rings. These, recall, were defined in
Definition
4

on page
4
of
Section
1.1
.
For brevity, we use the following notation:

Definition 13
.

Let
R
be a ring. I write:

•
\mathscr{S}\left(R\right)
, to denote the set of all
seminorms on
R
.

•
\mathscr{N}\left(R\right)
, to denote the set of all
norms on
R
.

•
\mathscr{V}\left(R\right)
, to denote the set of all
absolute values on
R
.

•
\mathscr{M}\left(R\right)
, to denote the set of all
multiplicative seminorms on
R
; this is known as the
Berkovich
spectrum of
R
.

Here, then, is the definition of a frame:

Definition 14
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a set and let
R
be a ring. Then, an
R
-frame
on
\mathcal{X}
,
denoted
\mathcal{F}
, consists of the following
data:

I. A set
D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, called
the
domain
of
\mathcal{F}
. (Many times, we will have
D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)=\mathcal{X}
,
but there will be cases where
\mathcal{X}
is a measure space and

\mathcal{X}\backslash D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
is a set of measure
zero.)

II. A map
\mathcal{F}:D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{S}\left(R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}\right)

which sends each
x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
to a seminorm
\mathcal{F}_{x}:R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}\rightarrow\left[0,\infty\right)
,
where
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}
is the ring of
R
-valued
functions on
D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
.

We say a frame is
multiplicative
when
\mathcal{F}_{x}\in\mathscr{M}\left(R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}\right)

for all
x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
. We say
\mathcal{F}
is

evaluative
whenever, for each
x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
,
there is an absolute value
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}\in\mathscr{V}\left(R\right)

so that:

(3.3)

\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(f\right)=\left|f\left(x\right)\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)},\textrm{ }\forall f\in R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}

Assumption 5
.

HENCEFORTH, UNLESS STATED OTHERWISE, ALL FRAMES WILL BE MULTIPLICATIVE
AND EVALUATIVE.

Remark 23
.

Aside from making things easier overall, the multiplicativity of
\mathcal{F}_{x}

guarantees that the only elements
f\in R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}

with
\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(f\right)=1
are the units of
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}
.
Moreover, viewing elements of
R
as constant
R
-valued functions
on
D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
, we then have that, for any
r\in R
,

\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(r\right)\neq 1
implies
r\notin R^{\times}
.
We’ll discuss the assumption of the evaluativity of
\mathcal{F}

momentarily.

As
\mathcal{F}_{x}
is a seminorm, it is not guaranteed that
f

will be the zero element of
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}
simply
when
\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(f\right)=0
; that is,
\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}

is not guaranteed to be trivial. However, since
\mathcal{F}
is
evaluative, we see that
\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}
is precisely
the set of
f\in R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}
which vanish at
x
.
Because of this, the ring quotient
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}/\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}

is isomorphic to
R
itself.

Definition 15
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an evaluative
R
-frame on
\mathcal{X}
.

I. For each
x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
, let
R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}

denote the metric completion of
R
with respect to the absolute
value
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}
.
Note
:
for compactness of notation, we will often write
\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)

to denote
R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}
.

II. We write
C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)

to denote the
space
of functions

compatible with
\mathcal{F}
.

This
is defined by the direct product:

(3.4)

C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}

We view elements of
C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
as functions
f

on
D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
with the property that
f\left(x\right)\in R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}

for all
x\in X
. We can make this view rigorous by defining
I\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
,
called the
image of
\mathcal{F}
, as:

(3.5)

I\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\bigcup_{x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}

Then, we have:

(3.6)

C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)=\left\{f:D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\rightarrow I\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\mid f\left(x\right)\in R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)},\textrm{ }\forall x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\right\}

Observe that
C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
is then an
R
-algebra
under scalar multiplication, with pointwise addition and multiplication.

Note that because
\mathcal{F}
is assumed to be evaluative, we can
make sense of
\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(f\right)
for all
f\in C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
;
this is simply
\left|f\left(x\right)\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}
,
where, in an acceptable abuse of notation,
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}

here denotes the extension of the absolute value of that name on
R

to one on the completion
R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}
.

Remark 24
.

In this paper, we use the hypothesis that
\mathcal{F}
is evaluative
in order to guarantee that
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}/\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}\cong R
.
Without assuming the evaluativity of
\mathcal{F}
, all of the definitions
given above hold verbatim, with the exception that instead of denoting
an absolute value on
R
, the symbol
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}

instead denotes the absolute value induced by
\mathcal{F}_{x}
on
the quotient
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}/\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}
,
which in the general case need not be isomorphic to
R
. Consequently,

R_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}
is not the completion of
R
with
respect to
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}
, but
the completion of
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}/\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}
.
Provided that modification is made, everything else in the above follows
without change.

In terms of how it affects the results of this paper, if we remove
the assumption that
\mathcal{F}
is evaluative, everything we are
doing will hold provided that we make the assumption that for every

f\in C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
and every
\epsilon>0
, we have
a non-empty intersection:

(3.7)

\bigcap_{x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}\pi_{x}^{-1}\left(U_{x}\left(f;\epsilon\right)\right)\neq\varnothing

Here,
\pi_{x}:R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}\rightarrow R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}/\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}

is the canonical projection, while:

(3.8)

U_{x}\left(f;\epsilon\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\left\{r\in R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}/\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}:\left|\pi_{x}\left(r\right)-f\left(x\right)\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}<\epsilon\right\}

where, as mentioned above,
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(x\right)}

is the absolute value induced by
\mathcal{F}_{x}
on
R^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}/\textrm{Ker}\mathcal{F}_{x}
.
Also, some of the notation given below will need to be modified slightly.
I intend to publish a paper on these and other details of frame theory
in the future.

Definition 16
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an evaluative frame. We say a sequence
\left\{f_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 0}\in C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)

converges to
f\in C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
with respect
to
\mathcal{F}
(a.k.a., the
f_{n}
s
\mathcal{F}
-converge
to
\mathcal{F}
; the
f_{n}
s converge frame-wise to
f
)
whenever:

(3.9)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(f_{n}-f\right)\overset{\mathbb{R}}{=}0,\textrm{ }\forall x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)

We denote this by writing:

(3.10)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f_{n}\left(x\right)\overset{\mathcal{F}}{=}f\left(x\right)

Also, we say a series of compatible functions
\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{n}

is
absolutely convergent with respect to
\mathcal{F}

/
is
absolutely
\mathcal{F}
-convergent
whenever:

(3.11)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(f_{n}\right)

converges in
\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
.

When working with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we will want our frames to be
compatible with both the profinite structure of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}

and the action of the shift operator
\theta_{p}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Definition 17
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an
R
-frame on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. We say
\mathcal{F}

is
reffinite
whenever:

I.
\mathbb{N}_{0}\subseteq D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
.

II.
\theta_{p}\left(D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)\right)\subseteq D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
.

III. For any
\mathfrak{z}\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
,
the absolute values
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)}

and
\left|\cdot\right|_{\mathcal{F}\left(\theta_{p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)}

are the same.

Assumption 6
.

HENCEFORTH, UNLESS STATED OTHERWISE, ALL FRAMES ARE ASSUMED TO BE
REFFINITE.

We will also need to explicate how frames interact with quotients
of the underlying ring.

Definition 18
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an
R
-frame on
\mathcal{X}
, and let
I

be an ideal of
R
. Then,
\mathcal{F}
induces a canonical
R/I
-frame
on
\mathcal{X}
, denoted
\mathcal{F}/I
, by the rule that, for
each
x\in D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
,
\left(\mathcal{F}/I\right)_{x}

is the multiplicative seminorm on
\left(R/I\right)^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}
defined
by:

(3.12)

\left(\mathcal{F}/I\right)_{x}\left(f\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\inf_{g\in I^{D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)}}\mathcal{F}_{x}\left(f+g\right)

We call this the
quotient of
\mathcal{F}
by
I
, and
refer to
\mathcal{F}/I
as a
quotient frame
.

Next, we have the notion of rising-continuity.

Definition 19
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
R
-frame on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
so that
\mathbb{N}_{0}\subseteq D\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
.
Then, we say a function
f\in C\left(\mathcal{F}\right)
is
\mathcal{F}
-rising-continuous

/
rising-continuous with respect to
\mathcal{F}
whenever:

(3.13)

f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\overset{\mathcal{F}}{=}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

This generalizes the notion of rising-continuity as defined in my
dissertation, where, for a metrically complete valued field
\mathbb{F}
,
a function
f:\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}
was said to be
rising-continuous whenever
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)

converged to
f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
in the topology of
\mathbb{F}

for all
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

I originally introduced the notion of rising-continuity in my dissertation,
where I devoted a subsection (Section 3.2) to covering its basic properties

[
16
]
, though traces of the idea seem to have been
known since at least Schikhof’s time. A notion of rising-continuity
occurs, though not by that name, in Exercises 62A and 62B in Part
3 of Chapter 6 of Schikhof’s
Ultrametric Calculus
, as part
of his discussion of the van der Put basis
[
12
]
.

In between the writing of my dissertation and the writing of this
paper, I have succeeded in giving a categorical description of the
property of rising-continuity. The details are given below for the
interested reader, though they will not be needed for this paper,
except at the end of
Section
5.3
,
where they become relevant to some of the speculation there.

Definition 20
.

A
profinite set
, also known as a
profinite space

or
Stone space
is a topological space which is homeomorphic
to the projective/inverse limit in the category of topological spaces
of a projective/inverse system of finite topological spaces, each
of which is equipped with the discrete topology. The index set of
this projective system is required to be
directed from below
,
meaning that for every
i,j\in I
, there exists
k\in I
so that

k\leq i
and
k\leq j
.

We write
\mathbf{ProS}

to denote the category of profinite
spaces. The objects of this category are profinite spaces; the morphisms
are continuous maps.

A
light profinite space/set
is a profinite space whose projective
system’s index set is
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\textrm{op}}
, meaning that
the projective system looks like:

(3.14)

A_{0}\overset{f_{0,1}}{\leftarrow}A_{1}\overset{f_{1,2}}{\leftarrow}A_{2}\overset{f_{2,3}}{\leftarrow}\cdots

We write
\mathbf{LProS}
to denote the category of light
profinite spaces. The objects of this category are light profinite
spaces; the morphisms are continuous maps.

Despite the simplicity of the rising continuity condition:

(3.15)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)=f\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

it does not seem to have been given systematic treatment in the literature.
In hindsight, this is not entirely surprising. I have has shown that
the “natural” context for rising-continuity appears to be profinite
sets that possess an additional direct limit structure, one that is
compatible with their profinite structure.

Definition 21
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. A
bidirectional system
in

\mathcal{C}
consists of:

I. A poset
I
with a partial order, denoted
\leq
.

II. A collection of objects
\left\{A_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
in

\mathcal{C}
.

III. For every
i,j\in I
with
i\leq j
, a morphism (called a

projection
)

\pi_{i,j}:A_{j}\rightarrow A_{i}
so that:

i. In the case where
i=j
, the morphism
\pi_{i,i}
is
\textrm{Id}_{i}
,
the identity morphism on
A_{i}
(i.e.
\textrm{Id}_{i}:A_{i}\rightarrow A_{i}
).

ii. For all
i,j,k\in I
with
i\leq j\leq k
:

(3.16)

\pi_{i,k}=\pi_{i,j}\circ\pi_{j,k}

That is, we have a commutative diagram:

(3.17)

\begin{array}[]{cccc}&\pi_{j,k}\\
A_{k}&\rightarrow&A_{j}\\
&\searrow&\downarrow&\pi_{i,j}\\
\pi_{i,k}&&A_{i}\end{array}

IV. For every
i,j\in I
with
i\leq j
, a morphism (called a

injection
)

\iota_{i,j}:A_{i}\rightarrow A_{j}
so that:

i. In the case where
i=j
,
\iota_{i,i}=\textrm{Id}_{i}

ii. For all
i,j,k\in I
with
i\leq j\leq k
:

(3.18)

\iota_{i,k}=\iota_{j,k}\circ\iota_{i,j}

That is, we have a commutative diagram:

(3.19)

\begin{array}[]{cccc}&\iota_{j,k}\\
A_{k}&\leftarrow&A_{j}\\
&\nwarrow&\uparrow&\iota_{i,j}\\
\iota_{i,k}&&A_{i}\end{array}

V. The projections and injections satisfy the relations:

(3.20)

\pi_{i,j}\circ\iota_{i,j}=\textrm{Id}_{i}

for all
i,j\in I
with
i\leq j
. Moreover:

(3.21)

\pi_{i,\infty}\circ\iota_{i,\infty}=\textrm{Id}_{i}

I then define the following:

Definition 22
.

A
reffinite set / reffinite space
(or
ref-finite
,
or
split profinite
)

S
is a profinite space obtained
by taking the projective limit of a bidirectional system of finite
discrete topological spaces whose index set is directed from below.
we write
\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(S\right)
to denote the injective limit
of the bidirectional system associated to
S
, and we write
S^{\prime}

to denote the set of all elements of
S
which are not in
\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(S\right)
.
We write
\mathbf{RefS}
to denote the category of reffinite space;
this is the category whose objects are reffinite spaces and whose
morphisms are continuous maps that respect the definite structure,
in the sense that for any reffinite sets
R,S
and any morphism
\psi:R\rightarrow S

of reffinite sets:

(3.22)

\displaystyle\psi\left(\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(R\right)\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(S\right)

(3.23)

\displaystyle\psi\left(R^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq S^{\prime}

Remark 25
.

The name “reffinite” is based on the formula
reflexive

+
finite
. Profinite objects are the projective limits of things,
while ind-finite objects are the injective limits of things, and these
two types of objects are categorically dual to one another. Since
objects which are self-dual are called reflexive, it seems reasonable
to unite profinite and ind-finite as “reffinite”.

Definition 23
.

Given a reffinite space
S
, the
rising topology
on
S

is the topology generated by the following sets:

I. Sets which are open in the profinite topology on
S
. (We call
these
profinite-open sets
.)

II. Singletons
\left\{s\right\}
, where
s
is any element of

\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(S\right)
. (We call these sets
point
sets
.)

III. Sets of the form
\left\{\mathfrak{s}\right\}\cup\left\{\pi_{j,\infty}\left(\mathfrak{s}\right):j\geq i\right\}
,
where
\mathfrak{s}
is any element of
S^{\prime}
and
i
is
any element of
I
. (We call these sets
dust sets
.)

Furthermore, given a topological space
T
, a function
\chi:S\rightarrow T

is said to be
rising-continuous
if it is continuous as a
map from
S
(equipped with its rising topology) to
T
. That is,

\chi^{-1}\left(U\right)
is open in
S
’s rising topology for all
open sets in
T
. We write
C^{\textrm{rise}}\left(S,T\right)
to
denote the space of rising-continuous functions from
S
to
T
.

Remark 26
.

Note that point sets and dust sets are both open and closed in the
rising topology.

The following propositions are then easily proven:

Proposition 16
.

Let
R
be a reffinite set. Then
\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(R\right)

is dense subset of
R
in both the profinite and rising topology.

Proposition 17
.

Let
T
be a topological space, let
S
be a reffinite set, and
let
\chi:S\rightarrow T
be rising-continuous. Then, for any reffinite
set
R
, and any continuous map
\psi:R\rightarrow S
(i.e., continuous
w.r.t. the spaces’ profinite topologies) satisfying:

(3.24)

\displaystyle\psi\left(\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(R\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{N}_{0}\left(S\right)

(3.25)

\displaystyle\psi\left(R^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq S^{\prime}

the composite
\chi\circ\psi:R\rightarrow T
is rising-continuous.

The standard example of such maps are as follows:

Definition 24
.

Let
p
and
q
be distinct primes.
Define the function
\psi_{q,p}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}

by:

(3.26)

\psi_{q,p}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}p^{n}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}q^{n}

I call
\psi_{q,p}
the
\left(p,q\right)
-adic space-change
map
.

Proposition 18
.

Let
p
and
q
be primes,
possibly non-distinct.

I.
\psi_{q,p}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is uniformly
continuous for all
p
and
q
; in particular, it is Hölder continuous
with exponent
\log_{p}q=\frac{\ln q}{\ln p}
, with:

(3.27)

\left|\psi_{q,p}\left(\mathfrak{a}\right)-\psi_{q,p}\left(\mathfrak{b}\right)\right|_{q}\leq\left|\mathfrak{a}-\mathfrak{b}\right|_{p}^{\log_{p}q}

This inequality holds with equality for all
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}

if and only if
p\leq q
.

II.
\psi_{q,p}
is injective if and only if
p\leq q
, and is surjective
if and only if
p\geq q
.

III.
\psi_{q,p}
is the unique rising-continuous function satisfying
the functional equations:

(3.28)

\psi_{q,p}\left(p\mathfrak{z}+j\right)=j+q\psi_{q,p}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right),\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},\textrm{ }\forall j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

Remark 27
.

Precomposing a
p
-adic F-series
X
with
\psi_{p,q}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}

then gives a
q
-adic F-series
X\circ\psi_{p,q}
.

Importantly, the existence of the Fourier transform of an F-series
remains unchanged when we precompose the F-series by a space-change
map.

Example 5
.

Given a
p
-adic F-series
X
characterized by:

(3.29)

X\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)=a_{k}X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{k},\textrm{ }\forall k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\},\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

let
Y
denote
X\circ\psi_{p,q}
. Then:

\displaystyle Y\left(q\mathfrak{z}+j\right)

\displaystyle=X\left(\psi_{p,q}\left(q\mathfrak{z}+j\right)\right)

\displaystyle=X\left(p\psi_{p,q}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+j\right)

\displaystyle=a_{j}X\left(\psi_{p,q}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right)+b_{j}

\displaystyle=a_{j}Y\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{j}

for all
j\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
. Thus, we can use the
formula from
Theorem
2

to
compute Fourier transforms for
X
and
Y
over their respective
spaces.

This suggests one might be able to view F-series as functors out of,
say, the category of light profinite abelian groups, in the manner
of Clausen & Scholze’s condensed mathematics, due to the passing
resemblance that this set-up has to theirs
[
14
]
. This
might present an avenue for realizing F-series as curves, in the manner
discussed in final subsections of
[
17
]
.

Finally, for completeness’ sake (pun intended), it is worth discussing
the adèlic aspects of frames and, more generally, completions of locally
convex topological rings. Indeed despite their apparent novelty, there
is a sense in which frames are a natural construction, especially
from a number theoretic perspective. To see this, however, we must
take up the language of
locally convex topological spaces
.
This is an extensive area of functional analysis, one that can be
applied to a host of different algebraic structures (algebras, rings,
vector spaces, modules, groups, etc.), united by the same underlying
idea, which we recall for the reader’s edification in the case where
the underlying structure is a ring.

Definition 25
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a topological ring (not necessarily unital or
commutative), let
I
be an indexing set, and let
\mathscr{P}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\left\{p_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}

be a collection of seminorms on
\mathcal{X}
. For any
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
any real number
\epsilon>0
, and any
i\in I
, the open ball centered
at
x
of radius
\epsilon
with respect to
p_{i}
is defined
by the set:

(3.30)

B_{i}\left(x;\epsilon\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\left\{y\in\mathcal{X}:p_{i}\left(y-x\right)<\epsilon\right\}

The
locally convex topology
(LCT)
on
\mathcal{X}

induced/generated by
\mathscr{P}
is the topology generated by
arbitrary unions and finite intersections of the
B_{i}\left(x;\epsilon\right)
.
A sequence
\left\{x_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{X}
is
said to
converge
to
x\in\mathcal{X}
with respect to
\mathscr{P}

(i.e., in the topology induced by
\mathscr{P}
) precisely when:

(3.31)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p_{i}\left(x-x_{n}\right)\overset{\mathbb{R}}{=}0,\textrm{ }\forall i\in I

A
locally convex topological ring
(LCTR) is a topological
ring equipped with an LCT.

Furthermore, a sequence
\left\{x_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{X}

is said to be
Cauchy
with respect to
\mathscr{P}
whenever,
for every
\epsilon>0
and every
i\in I
, there is an integer
N_{i}\geq 0

so that:

(3.32)

p_{i}\left(x_{m}-x_{n}\right)<\epsilon,\textrm{ }\forall m,n\geq N_{i}

A locally convex topology is said to be
complete
if every
Cauchy sequence converges. A LCTR is said to be
complete

if its LCT is complete. Every locally convex topological ring has
a completion which is unique up to isomorphism; this completion is
defined in the usual way as the collection of equivalence classes
of Cauchy sequences.

An
isomorphism
of LCTRs is an isomorphism of the underlying
rings which is also continuous with respect to the LCT.

For this we need to recall how to take the maximum of a collection
of finitely many seminorms.

Definition 26
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{N}\in\mathscr{S}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
. Then,
I write
\max\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{N}\right\}
to denote the map

\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\left[0,\infty\right)
defined by:

\max\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{N}\right\}\left(x\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\max_{1\leq n\leq N}p_{n}\left(x\right),\textrm{ }\forall x\in\mathcal{X}

Proposition 19
.

If
\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{N}\in\mathscr{S}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
,
then
\max\left\{\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{N}\right\}\in\mathscr{S}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
.
Also, the maximum of finitely many ring norms is a ring norm.

Proof: An easy exercise.
Q.E.D.

We also have the following fundamental observation:

Proposition 20
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a ring, and let
\mathscr{P}=\left\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{d}\right\}

be a set of finitely many norms on
\mathcal{X}
. Then, the following
topologies on
\mathcal{X}
are equal:

I. The metric topology induced by the norm:

(3.33)

\left\|x\right\|\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\nu_{i}\left(x\right),\textrm{ }\forall x\in\mathcal{X}

II. The locally convex topology induced by
\mathscr{P}
.

Proof: A sequence in
\mathcal{X}
converges in (I) if and only if
it converges in (II). The finiteness of
\mathscr{P}
is essential
to this argument. More specifically, identify
\mathcal{X}
with
its image in the cartesian product
\mathcal{X}^{d}
under the diagonal
embedding
x\mapsto\left(x,\ldots,x\right)
. Then, equip the
i
th
coordinate of this embedding with the norm
\nu_{i}
and give the
entire embedding the product topology. Then, the product topology,
(I), and (II) are all equivalent.

Q.E.D.

The number theoretic connection comes by way of the
Weak
Approximation Theorem
of algebraic number theory.

Theorem 4

(The Weak Approximation Theorem
[
7
]
)
.

Let
K
be any field, let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, and let
\left|\cdot\right|_{1},\ldots,\left|\cdot\right|_{N}

be inequivalent, non-trivial absolute values on
K
. Choose any
N

elements
a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\in K
. Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,
there exists an
a\in K
so that
\left|a-a_{n}\right|_{n}<\epsilon

for all
n\in\left\{1,\ldots,N\right\}
.

Before we use this, however, we need to establish the following:

Corollary 2
.

Let
R
be a ring, and let
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{d}
be finitely
many inequivalent, non-trivial norms on
R
, and let
\alpha
be
their maximum:

(3.34)

\alpha\left(r\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\nu_{n}\left(r\right),\textrm{ }\forall r\in R

Finally, let
\hat{R}
denote the metric completion of
R
with
respect to
\alpha
.

In this situation,
\hat{R}
is isometric to
\prod_{n=1}^{N}R_{n}
,
the metric space given by taking the cartesian product of the
R_{n}
s,
where for each
n
,
R_{n}
is the completion of
K
with respect
to
\nu_{n}
. The norm on the product is:

(3.35)

\left\|\mathbf{z}\right\|\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\nu_{n}\left(z_{i}\right)

Proof: An element of
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
is a tuple of the form

\left(r_{1},\ldots,r_{d}\right)
where, for each
n\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
,

r_{n}\in R_{n}
. Now, since
R
is dense in each
R_{n}
, for
each
n\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
, there exists a sequence
\left\{a_{m,n}\right\}_{m\geq 1}\in K

so that
\nu_{n}\left(r_{n}-a_{m,n}\right)<1/m
for all
m\geq 1

and all
n\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
. By the
Weak Approximation
Theorem
, for each
m
, there is a
b_{m}\in K
so that
\nu_{n}\left(a_{m,n}-b_{m}\right)<1/m

for all
n\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
. Hence, for all
m
and

n
, we have:

\nu_{n}\left(r_{n}-b_{m}\right)\leq\nu_{n}\left(r_{n}-a_{m,n}\right)+\nu_{n}\left(a_{m,n}-b_{m}\right)<\frac{1}{2m}

Thus, the sequence
\left\{b_{m}\right\}_{m\geq 1}
is a sequence
in
R
which, for each
n
, converges in
R_{n}
to
r_{n}
as

m\rightarrow\infty
.

Now, consider
R
as a metric space with
\alpha
as its metric,
consider the diagonal embedding:
\phi:R\rightarrow R^{d}
defined
by:

(3.36)

\phi\left(r\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\underbrace{\left(r,\ldots,r\right)}_{N\textrm{ times}}

Note that we can make
R^{d}
a metric space by defining
\delta:R^{d}\times R^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

by:

(3.37)

\delta\left(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{s}\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\nu_{n}\left(r_{n}-s_{n}\right)

where
\mathbf{r}=\left(r_{1},\ldots,r_{d}\right)
and
\mathbf{s}=\left(s_{1},\ldots,s_{d}\right)

are elements of
R^{d}
. Let
\Delta\left(R^{d}\right)
denote the
image of
\phi
in
R^{d}
. As constructed,
\phi
is an isometry
from
\left(R,\alpha\right)
onto
\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)
.
Thus, the completion of
\left(R,\alpha\right)
will be isometric
to the completion of
\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)
.
Now, as we did above, pick:

(3.38)

\mathbf{r}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\left(r_{1},\ldots,r_{d}\right)\in\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}

Then, there exists a sequence
\left\{b_{m}\right\}_{m\geq 1}
in

R
so that:

(3.39)

\nu_{n}\left(r_{n}-b_{m}\right)<\frac{1}{2m},\textrm{ }\forall m\geq 1,\forall n\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}

Consequently, letting
\mathbf{b}_{m}
be the element of
\Delta\left(R^{d}\right)

whose every entry is
b_{m}
(ex.,
\mathbf{b}_{1}
is a tuple of

d
consecutive
b_{1}
s), observe that for any
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\delta\left(\mathbf{b}_{m_{1}},\mathbf{b}_{m_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\nu_{n}\left(b_{m_{1}}-b_{m_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\nu_{n}\left(b_{m_{1}}-z_{n}\right)+\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\nu_{n}\left(z_{n}-b_{m_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2m_{1}}+\frac{1}{2m_{2}}

Thus, for any
\epsilon>0
, choose
\min\left\{m_{1},m_{2}\right\}\geq\frac{1}{\epsilon}
.
Then:

(3.40)

\delta\left(\mathbf{b}_{m_{1}},\mathbf{b}_{m_{2}}\right)<\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}<\epsilon

which shows that
\left\{\mathbf{b}_{m}\right\}_{m\geq 1}
is Cauchy
in
\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)
. Since
\delta

extends to a metric on
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
, any completion of

\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)
is necessarily contained
in any completion of
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
. In particular, since

\mathbf{r}\in\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
, and:

(3.41)

\delta\left(\mathbf{r},\mathbf{b}_{m}\right)=\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\nu_{n}\left(z_{n}-b_{m}\right)<\frac{1}{2m}

we conclude that
\mathbf{b}_{m}
converges to
\mathbf{r}
in
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
.

Since
\mathbf{r}
was arbitrary, we have that there is an isometric
copy of
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
contained within the completion of

\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)
, and hence, contained
within the completion of
\left(R,\alpha\right)
.

To finish, we just need to show that every element of
\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)
’s
completion is contained in
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
. For this, we first
note that since
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
is a cartesian product of
finitely many complete metric spaces (the
R_{n}
s), it itself is
a complete metric space under
\delta
. Since
\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)

embeds in
\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
, we then have that the completion
of
\left(\Delta\left(R^{d}\right),\delta\right)
is contained in

\prod_{n=1}^{d}R_{n}
, and we are done.

Q.E.D.

A simple modification of this argument lets us work with
completions of the ring of functions
X\rightarrow K
, in a manner
that is clearly frame-theoretic.

Corollary 3
.

Let
X
be a set, let
K
be a field, and let
R
be the ring of
functions
X\rightarrow K
under the standard pointwise operations.
Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
, let
\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{d}
be inequivalent,
non-trivial norms values on
K
, and for each
n\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
,
let
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{n}
be the supremum norm:

(3.42)

\left\|f\right\|_{n}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\sup_{x\in X}\nu_{n}\left(f\left(x\right)\right)

Then:

(3.43)

\alpha\left(f\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\max_{1\leq n\leq d}\left\|f\right\|_{n}

induces a norm on
R
, and the completion of
R
with respect to

\alpha
is then a complete metric ring which is ring isometric to:

(3.44)

\left(\prod_{n=1}^{d}K_{n}\right)^{X}

the ring of functions from
X
to
\prod_{n=1}^{d}K_{n}
.

Because of this, we see that frames and, more generally, locally convex
topologies, provide a natural method of keeping track of sequences
of objects (numbers, functions, etc.) whose convergence occurs in
potentially multiple different completions of a given ring. Although
I originally devised frames as a purely ad hoc device to simplify
discussion of convergence behavior of this type
[
16
]
,
it is clear that we can turn this ad hoc aspect of the construction
on its head to capture arithmetic information. In particular, we can
consider projective systems of frames, where a frame
\mathcal{F}

comes before another frame
\mathcal{G}
if every
\mathcal{G}
-convergent
sequence is also
\mathcal{F}
-convergent. By considering the inverse
limit of this system, one ought to get something very adèle-adjacent,
if not fully adèlic, possibly with the requirement that we impose
a restriction condition in order to guarantee that the limit frame
generates adélic completions. In the spirit of
Example
4
,
this suggests that perhaps the “correct” way to deal with pointwise
convergence of F-series whose parameters are all scalars is by considering
the image of the F-series under the diagonal embedding into an appropriately
constructed frame-theoretic version of the adèle ring of a global
field, though questions remain as to how to interpret an F-series
in this way when, at a given
\mathfrak{z}
, there might exist places
of the underlying global field for which
X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

is either non-convergent, or divergent to
\infty
in absolute value.
I discuss this a bit in
[
15
]
, suggesting that projective
spaces might be used to deal with divergences, though I don’t think
this technical detail will be resolved that easily.

On the other side of things—domain-wise—in viewing
F-series as functors out of profinite abelian groups, just as we can
realize
X
’s image as being adèle-valued by way of the diagonal
embedding, we might be able to realize F-series as functions out of
rings of finite adèles (direct products of all the possible completions
of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
with respect to every non-trivial place of
K
)
by defining, the image of
X
on
\mathcal{O}_{K_{\ell}}
, for every
place
\ell
of
K
, by the composite
X\circ\psi_{p,\ell}
, where

\psi_{p,\ell}:\mathcal{O}_{K_{\ell}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is
the space-change map. In this way, we would have
X
as being from
the adèles to the adèles, capturing all possible arithmetic information
one could get. Figuring out these details is just one of the many
avenues of future investigations my work opens onto.

3.2.
Ascent from Fractional Ideals
of Polynomial Rings

At the heart of our work is the idea of a
p
-adic F-series, an object

X
satisfying equations of the shape:

(3.45)

X\left(p\mathfrak{z}+k\right)=a_{k}X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)+b_{k},\textrm{ }\forall k\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

for objects
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
, and where
\mathfrak{z}
is a

p
-adic variable. Obviously, the nature and extent of
X
’s existence
depend what kinds of objects we are talking about.

In my dissertation and subsequent research, even in the case when
the
a_{k}
s and
b_{k}
s were elements of a field, I frequently
treated them as indeterminates, and found that all of the analysis
worked out without issue. I am pleased to report that this informal
procedure can be made rigorous.

To begin with, if we treat the
a_{k}
s and
b_{k}
s as formal indeterminates,
evaluating (
3.45
) at
\mathfrak{z}=0
and setting

k=0
gives us:

(3.46)

X\left(0\right)=a_{0}X\left(0\right)+b_{0}

which we can then solve to obtain:

(3.47)

X\left(0\right)=\frac{b_{0}}{1-a_{0}}

Setting
\mathfrak{z}=0
in (
3.45
) but letting
k

vary, we would then get:

(3.48)

X\left(k\right)=a_{k}X\left(0\right)+b_{k}=\frac{a_{k}b_{0}}{1-a_{0}}+b_{k}

Writing:

(3.49)

X\left(pk_{2}+k_{1}\right)=a_{k_{1}}X\left(k_{2}\right)+b_{k_{1}},\textrm{ }\forall k_{1},k_{2}\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

we then see that the value of
X
on
\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}

determine the values of
X
on
\left\{p,\ldots,p^{2}-1\right\}
.
More generally, for any
n\geq 1
, the values of
X
on
\left\{p^{n-1},\ldots,p^{n}-1\right\}

then completely determine the values of
X
on
\left\{p^{n},\ldots,p^{n+1}-1\right\}
,
and thus, that the existence of the right-hand side of (
3.47
)
is sufficient to guarantee that
X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)
is uniquely
defined for all
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Moreover, note that for any
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

will be a rational function in the
a_{k}
s and
b_{k}
s with coefficients
in
\mathbb{Z}
, and that, crucially,
\left(1-a_{0}\right)X\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)

will always be an element of
\mathbb{Z}\left[a_{0},\ldots,a_{p-1},b_{0},\ldots,b_{p-1}\right]
.
This shows that
X\mid_{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
takes values in a fractional
ideal of
\mathbb{Z}\left[a_{0},\ldots,a_{p-1},b_{0},\ldots,b_{p-1}\right]
.
For technical reasons, however, rather than use fractional ideals,
we will instead need to use localizations.

As is classical in commutative algebra and algebraic geometry, given
a ring of polynomials, we can impose relations on the polynomials’
variables by modding out by an ideal. Viewing
X
as module-valued
over
\mathbb{N}_{0}
allows us to impose nearly any relations or
evaluations on the
a_{k}
s and
b_{k}
s as we wish by simply taking
an appropriate quotient of
X
’s target space. The one caveat to
note is that, in order to get (
3.47
) from
(
3.46
) it must be that the relation

a_{0}=1
does not hold, else (
3.47
) becomes
division by zero. That being said, note that if
a_{0}=1
, then (
3.46
)
forces the relation
b_{0}=0
to hold. In this case, we are then
free to choose any value for
X\left(0\right)
, after which the recursive
structure of (
3.45
) will guarantee that (
3.45
)
will have a unique solution on
\mathbb{N}_{0}
for each chosen initial
condition.

In analogy with and generalization of the notion of an affine vector
space, we also have the notion of an affine subring.

Definition 27
.

Let
R
be a commutative unital ring. . Then, we call a set
A\subseteq M

an
affine subring
of
R
whenever, for any
b\in A
, the
set
\left\{a-b:a\in A\right\}
is a subring of
M
.

Remark 28
.

Just like with affine vector spaces, given
r\in R
and a subring

S
of
R
, the coset
r+S
is then an affine subring.

Our set-up is then as follows:

Proposition 21
.

Let
p
be an integer
\geq 2
, let
K

be a global field (if
\textrm{char}K>0
, assume
\textrm{char}K

is co-prime to
p
), let
a_{0},\ldots,a_{p-1}
and
b_{0},\ldots,b_{p-1}

be indeterminates, let
R
denote the polynomial ring
\mathcal{O}_{K}\left[a_{0},\ldots,a_{p-1},b_{0},\ldots,b_{p-1}\right]
,
and let
\mathcal{R}
denote the quotient:

(3.50)

\mathcal{R}\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\frac{R\left[x\right]}{\left\langle\left(1-a_{0}\right)x-1\right\rangle}

that is, the localization of
R
away from the prime ideal
\left\langle 1-a_{0}\right\rangle
.
Then, the functional equation:

(3.51)

X\left(n\right)=a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}X\left(\theta_{p}\left(n\right)\right)+b_{\left[n\right]_{p}},\textrm{ }\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

has a unique solution
X:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
.

Next, let
I
be an ideal of
R
. Then:

I. The solution
X:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
of (
3.51
)
induces a unique solution
X:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}

if and only if
\left\langle 1-a_{0}\right\rangle\nsubseteq I
;
here
X:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
is just
the image of
X:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}
under the canonical
projection
\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
.

II. If
\left\langle 1-a_{0}\right\rangle\subseteq I
and
\left\langle b_{0}\right\rangle\nsubseteq I
,
the set of all
X:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}

satisfying (
3.51
) is empty.

III. If
\left\langle 1-a_{0},b_{0}\right\rangle\subseteq I
, the
set of all
X:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}

satisfying (
3.51
) is an affine submodule of
\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}

of dimension
1
.

Proof: The only part needing explication is (III). So, letting
I

be as given, let
X,Y:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}

be solutions of (
3.51
). Then, for
Z=X-Y
:

\displaystyle Z\left(n\right)

\displaystyle=X\left(n\right)-Y\left(n\right)

\displaystyle=a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}X\left(\theta_{p}\left(n\right)\right)+b_{\left[n\right]_{p}}-\left(a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}Y\left(\theta_{p}\left(n\right)\right)+b_{\left[n\right]_{p}}\right)

\displaystyle=a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}\left(X\left(\theta_{p}\left(n\right)\right)-Y\left(\theta_{p}\left(n\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}Z\left(\theta_{p}\left(n\right)\right)

where all equalities are in
\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
. For
n\in\left\{0,\ldots,p-1\right\}
,
we obtain:

(3.52)

Z\left(n\right)=a_{n}Z\left(0\right)

Moreover, since
a_{0}=1
in
\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
, we see
that
Z\left(0\right)=a_{0}Z\left(0\right)=Z\left(0\right)
, which
leaves
Z\left(0\right)
undetermined. However, once
Z\left(0\right)

is chosen, that choice completely determines the value of
Z\left(n\right)

for all
n\geq 0
. So, given any two functions
Z_{1},Z_{2}:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}

so that:

(3.53)

Z_{j}\left(n\right)=a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}Z_{j}\left(n\right),\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 0

we see that, for any
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
:

(3.54)

\left(r_{1}Z_{1}+r_{2}Z_{2}\right)\left(n\right)=a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}\left(r_{1}Z_{1}+r_{2}Z_{2}\right)\left(n\right),\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 0

Hence, the set of
f:\mathbb{N}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}

satisfying the equation:

(3.55)

f\left(n\right)=a_{\left[n\right]_{p}}f\left(n\right),\textrm{ }\forall n\geq 0

is an
R
-subring of
\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
of dimension
1
.
Since
Z
was the difference of two
\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
-valued
solutions of (
3.51
), we conclude that the set of
\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
-valued
solutions of (
3.51
) is a one-dimensional affine subring of

\mathcal{R}/I\mathcal{R}
.

Q.E.D.

In light of this, we will use the following terminology
to describe our ideals.

Definition 28
.

Given
R
as in
Proposition
21
, we
say an ideal
I\subseteq R
is
admissible
if either:

I.
\left\langle 1-a_{0}\right\rangle\nsubseteq I
.

II.
\left\langle 1-a_{0},b_{0}\right\rangle\subseteq I

We say
I
is a
unique solution ideal
when (I) holds, and
say
I
is a
non-unique solution ideal
when (II) holds.

With
Proposition
21
now established,
we can freely consider the quotient of
\mathcal{R}
by any admissible
ideal. This formalizes the idea of treating the
a_{k}
s and
b_{k}
s
as indeterminates. With that being done, the next order of business
is to consider how to extend
X
from a function on
\mathbb{N}_{0}

to one on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
or a subset thereof. For this, we nest
equation (
3.51
)
N
times to obtain:

(3.56)

X\left(m\right)\overset{\mathcal{R}}{=}X\left(\theta_{p}^{\circ N}\left(m\right)\right)\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}a_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(m\right)\right]_{p}}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}b_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(m\right)\right]_{p}}\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}a_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ k}\left(m\right)\right]_{p}}

for all integers
m\geq 0
and where, as indicated, the equality occurs
in
\mathcal{R}
. Letting
\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
be arbitrary,
set
m=\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}
. Since
\theta_{p}^{\circ N}\left(m\right)=0

for all
m\in\left\{0,\ldots,p^{N}-1\right\}
, with the help of

Proposition
2
,

we get:

(3.57)

X\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)\overset{\mathcal{R}}{=}X\left(0\right)a_{0}^{N}\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{N}}\right)+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}b_{\left[\theta_{p}^{\circ n}\left(\mathfrak{z}\right)\right]_{p}}a_{0}^{n}\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right),\textrm{ }\forall N\geq 0,\textrm{ }\forall\mathfrak{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}

where
\kappa
denotes:

(3.58)

\kappa\left(m\right)\overset{\textrm{def}}{=}\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}\left(\frac{a_{k}}{a_{0}}\right)^{\#_{p:k}\left(m\right)},\textrm{ }\forall m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

Remark 29
.

Technically, it is an abuse of notation to write
\kappa
separately,
as
\kappa\left(m\right)\notin\mathcal{R}
. Rather, the correct function
to define is the M-function
\left\{a_{0}^{n}\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\right\}_{n\geq 0}
.
The presence of the factor
a_{0}^{n}
guarantees that
a_{0}^{n}\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\in\mathcal{R}

for all
n
and
\mathfrak{z}
. Indeed, we have:

(3.59)

a_{0}^{n}\kappa\left(\left[\mathfrak{z}\right]_{p^{n}}\right)\in R=\mathcal{O}_{K}\left[a_{0},\ldots,a_{p-1},b_{0},\ldots,b_{p-1}\right]

for all
n
and
\mathfrak{z}
.

The obvious thing to do is to take the limit of (
3.57
)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Not only would this extend/interpolate
